View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Harry Potter has a difficult homework. Given a rectangular table, consisting of n × m cells. Each cell of the table contains the integer. Harry knows how to use two spells: the first spell change the sign of the integers in the selected row, the second — in the selected column. Harry's task is to make non-negative the sum of the numbers in each row and each column using these spells.

Alone, the boy can not cope. Help the young magician!

Input

The first line contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 100) — the number of rows and the number of columns. 

Next n lines follow, each contains m integers: j-th integer in the i-th line is ai, j (|ai, j| ≤ 100), the number in the i-th row and j-th column of the table.

The rows of the table numbered from 1 to n. The columns of the table numbered from 1 to m.

Output

In the first line print the number a — the number of required applications of the first spell. Next print a space-separated integers — the row numbers, you want to apply a spell. These row numbers must be distinct!

In the second line print the number b — the number of required applications of the second spell. Next print b space-separated integers — the column numbers, you want to apply a spell. These column numbers must be distinct!

If there are several solutions are allowed to print any of them.

Examples

Input

4 1
-1
-1
-1
-1


Output

4 1 2 3 4 
0 


Input

2 4
-1 -1 -1 2
1 1 1 1


Output

1 1 
1 4 

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m table with integers
2. We can apply two spells:
   - First spell: flip the sign of all integers in a row
   - Second spell: flip the sign of all integers in a column
3. Goal: Make the sum of each row and each column non-negative

## Key Insights

1. Flipping a row changes the sign of all elements in that row
2. Flipping a column changes the sign of all elements in that column
3. The order of operations doesn't matter because flipping a row twice is equivalent to no flip, and same for columns.

## Strategy

The key insight is that we need to decide which rows and columns to flip. Let me think about this systematically:

1. For each row, if the sum is negative, we might want to flip it
2. For each column, if the sum is negative, we might want to flip it

However, there's a dependency: flipping a row affects the column sums, and vice versa.

Let me consider a greedy approach:
- Calculate the initial sum for each row and each column
- If a row sum is negative, flip that row
- After flipping rows, recalculate column sums
- If any column sum is negative, flip that column
- After flipping columns, check if all row and column sums are non-negative

But we need to be careful about the order. Actually, let's think differently:

What if we consider that we can flip any combination of rows and columns, and we want to minimize the total operations?

Actually, let me think of it this way:
- We can flip rows and columns independently
- The final sign of each element at position (i,j) will be determined by whether row i is flipped and column j is flipped
- If both row i and column j are flipped, the sign becomes the original sign
- If exactly one is flipped, the sign changes
- If neither or both are flipped, the sign remains the same

But we don't care about individual elements, only about row and column sums being non-negative.

## Better Approach

I'll use a greedy algorithm:
1. Go through each row. If the row sum is negative, flip that row
2. Then go through each column. If the column sum is negative, flip that column
3. This should work because:
   - Flipping a row changes only the row sum and affects column sums
   - But after we fix all rows, we can then fix column sums

Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: All -1 in a 4×1 table
- Row sums: [-1, -1, -1, -1] (all negative)
- Flip all rows: all become +1
- Row sums: [1, 1, 1, 1] (all positive)
- Column sums: [4] (positive)
- No column needs to be flipped
- Output: 4 rows to flip, 0 columns

Example 2: 
```
-1 -1 -1 2
1 1 1 1
```
- Row sums: [-2, 4] → Row 1 sum is negative, so flip row 1
- After flipping row 1: 
```
1 1 1 -2
1 1 1 1
```
- Row sums: [1, 4] (both non-negative)
- Column sums: [2, 2, 2, -1] → Column 4 sum is negative, so flip column 4
- After flipping column 4:
```
1 1 1 2
1 1 1 1
```
- Column sums: [2, 2, 2, 2] (all non-negative)
- Output: flip row 1, flip column 4

This matches the expected output!

## Algorithm

1. Read input
2. Calculate row sums and column sums
3. For each row i from 1 to n:
   - If row sum < 0, add row i to the flip list
4. For each column j from 1 to m:
   - Calculate column sum after applying row flips
   - If column sum < 0, add column j to the flip list
5. Output the results

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Read the table
table = []
for _ in range(n):
    row = list(map(int, input().split()))
    table.append(row)

# Calculate initial row sums and column sums
row_sums = [sum(table[i]) for i in range(n)]
col_sums = [sum(table[i][j] for i in range(n)) for j in range(m)]

# List to store which rows and columns to flip
rows_to_flip = []
cols_to_flip = []

# First, check each row - if sum is negative, flip it
for i in range(n):
    if row_sums[i] < 0:
        rows_to_flip.append(i + 1)  # Convert to 1-indexed

# Apply row flips to update the table and recalculate column sums
# We need to update the column sums after applying row flips
updated_col_sums = [0] * m
for j in range(m):
    for i in range(n):
        # Element at (i,j) will have sign flipped if row i is flipped
        if i + 1 in rows_to_flip:
            # Row i is flipped, so sign changes
            updated_col_sums[j] += -table[i][j]
        else:
            # Row i is not flipped
            updated_col_sums[j] += table[i][j]

# Check each column - if sum is negative, flip it
for j in range(m):
    if updated_col_sums[j] < 0:
        cols_to_flip.append(j + 1)  # Convert to 1-indexed

# Output the results
print(len(rows_to_flip), *rows_to_flip)
print(len(cols_to_flip), *cols_to_flip)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m table with integers in each cell
2. We can use two spells:
   - First spell: flip the sign of all integers in a selected row
   - Second spell: flip the sign of all integers in a selected column
3. Goal: Make the sum of each row and each column non-negative

## Key Insights

1. When we flip a row, we change the sign of all elements in that row
2. When we flip a column, we change the sign of all elements in that column
3. The order of operations doesn't matter because flipping a row twice is equivalent to no flip, and same for columns.

## Strategy

Let me think about this systematically:

1. For each row, if the sum is negative, we need to flip it (apply the first spell)
2. For each column, if the sum is negative after applying row flips, we need to flip it (apply the second spell)

However, there's a catch: flipping a row affects column sums too. So we need to be careful about the order.

Actually, let's think differently:

- We can think of this as: we want each row sum ≥ 0 and each column sum ≥ 0
- We can try to make all row sums non-negative first by flipping negative rows
- Then, for columns that still have negative sums, we flip them

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:** All elements are -1
- Row sums: all are -1 (negative)
- So we flip all rows → all become 1, row sums become +1 (good)
- Column sums: each column has 4 ones → sum = 4 (positive)
- No column needs to be flipped
- Output: 4 rows 1,2,3,4 and 0 columns

**Example 2:** 
```
-1 -1 -1 2
1 1 1 1
```
- Row 1 sum: -1-1-1+2 = -1 (negative) → flip row 1
- Row 2 sum: 1+1+1+1 = 4 (positive)
- After flipping row 1: 
  ```
  1 1 1 -2
  1 1 1 1
  ```
- Column sums:
  - Col 1: 1+1 = 2 (positive)
  - Col 2: 1+1 = 2 (positive) 
  - Col 3: 1+1 = 2 (positive)
  - Col 4: -2+1 = -1 (negative) → flip column 4
- After flipping col 4:
  ```
  1 1 1 2
  1 1 1 1
  ```
- All row and column sums are non-negative

Wait, let me check: after flipping row 1 and column 4:
- Row 1: 1+1+1+2 = 5 ≥ 0 ✓
- Row 2: 1+1+1+1 = 4 ≥ 0 ✓
- Col 1: 1+1 = 2 ≥ 0 ✓
- Col 2: 1+1 = 2 ≥ 0 ✓
- Col 3: 1+1 = 2 ≥ 0 ✓
- Col 4: 2+1 = 3 ≥ 0 ✓

Perfect!

## Algorithm

1. Read the input
2. Calculate initial row sums and column sums
3. For each row, if the sum is negative, add it to the row flips list
4. After applying row flips, recalculate column sums
5. For each column, if the sum is negative, add it to the column flips list
6. Output the results

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Read the grid
grid = []
for _ in range(n):
    row = list(map(int, input().split()))
    grid.append(row)

# Calculate initial row sums and column sums
row_sums = [sum(grid[i]) for i in range(n)]
col_sums = [0] * m
for j in range(m):
    for i in range(n):
        col_sums[j] += grid[i][j]

# Determine which rows to flip (if row sum is negative)
row_flips = []
for i in range(n):
    if row_sums[i] < 0:
        row_flips.append(i + 1)  # 1-indexed

# Apply row flips to the grid
for i in row_flips:
    for j in range(m):
        grid[i-1][j] = -grid[i-1][j]

# Recalculate column sums after row flips
col_sums = [0] * m
for j in range(m):
    for i in range(n):
        col_sums[j] += grid[i][j]

# Determine which columns to flip (if column sum is negative after row flips)
col_flips = []
for j in range(m):
    if col_sums[j] < 0:
        col_flips.append(j + 1)  # 1-indexed

# Output the result
print(len(row_flips), *row_flips)
print(len(col_flips), *col_flips)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have an n×m table with integers. We can apply two spells:
1. Change the sign of all integers in a selected row
2. Change the sign of all integers in a selected column

Goal: Make the sum of each row and each column non-negative.

## Key Insights

1. If we flip a row, we change the sign of all elements in that row
2. If we flip a column, we change the sign of all elements in that column
3. The order doesn't matter - flipping row i and then column j has the same effect as flipping column j and then row i

## Strategy

The key insight is that we can think about this problem in terms of the final signs of each element. Each element at position (i,j) will have a sign that depends on whether we flipped row i and/or column j.

Let's think about the sum of each row and column:
- For row i: sum = sum of all elements in row i with their final signs
- For column j: sum = sum of all elements in column j with their final signs

We want all row sums ≥ 0 and all column sums ≥ 0.

## Approach

A simple greedy approach:
1. For each row, calculate its sum without any flips
2. If a row sum is negative, we flip that row (this makes the row sum positive since we're flipping all signs)
3. After flipping rows, calculate column sums
4. If any column sum is negative, flip that column

Wait, let me reconsider. When we flip a row, we flip all elements in that row. When we flip a column, we flip all elements in that column. But flipping both row i and column j will result in the element (i,j) being flipped twice (back to original).

Actually, let me think of it differently:

The problem is equivalent to: can we assign signs to each row and column such that the resulting row sums and column sums are all non-negative?

Since we can flip any combination of rows and columns, for each element (i,j), its final value will be: original_value * (-1)^(row_i_flip + col_j_flip)

The sum of row i after all flips will be: sum_j [a[i][j] * (-1)^(row_i_flip + col_j_flip)]

This is equal to: (-1)^(row_i_flip) * sum_j [a[i][j] * (-1)^(col_j_flip)]

So the row sum for row i is (-1)^(row_i_flip) times the weighted sum of row i.

Similarly for columns.

A simpler approach: try all possible combinations of row flips and column flips? That's 2^n * 2^m which is too much.

Alternative insight: since we only care about making sums non-negative, we can use a greedy approach:

1. Initially, no flips
2. For each row, if the sum is negative, flip that row
3. For each column, if the sum is negative, flip that column

But this might not work because flipping a column affects all row sums.

Actually, let me implement a simpler approach:

Since the constraints are small (n, m ≤ 100), I can try the following:

- Start with no flips
- Go through each row and if the row sum is negative, flip it
- Then go through each column and if the column sum is negative, flip it
- This should work because flipping rows first ensures all row sums become positive, then column flips just adjust the column sums

Wait, this isn't guaranteed to work. Let me think of a better approach.

Actually, the problem has a known solution: we can always achieve non-negative row and column sums by systematically flipping rows and columns.

Let me implement the following algorithm:
1. Calculate initial sums for all rows and columns
2. While there exists a row with negative sum, flip that row
3. While there exists a column with negative sum, flip that column

This greedy approach should work because:
- When we flip a row with negative sum, its sum becomes positive
- After flipping rows, the column sums change, but we then check and flip columns with negative sums
- This process will eventually terminate

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
table = []
for _ in range(n):
    row = list(map(int, input().split()))
    table.append(row)

# We'll track which rows and columns we flip
row_flips = [False] * n
col_flips = [False] * m

# Calculate initial sums
row_sums = [sum(table[i][j] for j in range(m)) for i in range(n)]
col_sums = [sum(table[i][j] for i in range(n)) for j in range(m)]

# Greedy approach: keep flipping rows and columns with negative sums
while True:
    # Check if any row has negative sum
    row_flip_needed = False
    for i in range(n):
        # Calculate current sum for row i considering flips
        current_sum = sum(table[i][j] * (-1) ** (row_flips[i] + col_flips[j]) for j in range(m))
        if current_sum < 0:
            row_flips[i] = not row_flips[i]
            row_flip_needed = True
            break
    
    if not row_flip_needed:
        break
    
    # Check if any column has negative sum
    col_flip_needed = False
    for j in range(m):
        # Calculate current sum for column j considering flips
        current_sum = sum(table[i][j] * (-1) ** (row_flips[i] + col_flips[j]) for i in range(n))
        if current_sum < 0:
            col_flips[j] = not col_flips[j]
            col_flip_needed = True
            break
    
    if not col_flip_needed:
        break

# Output the results
row_to_apply = []
for i in range(n):
    if row_flips[i]:
        row_to_apply.append(i + 1)  # 1-indexed

col_to_apply = []
for j in range(m):
    if col_flips[j]:
        col_to_apply.append(j + 1)  # 1-indexed

print(len(row_to_apply), *row_to_apply)
print(len(col_to_apply), *col_to_apply)
```

        if current_sum < 0:
            col_flips[j] = not col_flips[j]

        break

Next n lines follow, each contains m integers: j-th integer in the i-th line is ai, j (|ai, j| ≤ 100), the number in the i-th row and j-th column of the table.

The rows of the table numbered from 1 to n. The columns of the table numbered from 1 to m.

Output

In the first line print the number a — the number of required applications of the first spell. Next print a space-separated integers — the row numbers, you want to apply a spell. These row numbers must be distinct!

In the second line print the number b — the number of required applications of the second spell. Next print b space-separated integers — the column numbers, you want to apply a spell. These column numbers must be distinct!

If there are several solutions are allowed to print any of them.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m table with integers
2. We can apply two spells:
   - First spell: flip the sign of all numbers in a selected row
   - Second spell: flip the sign of all numbers in a selected column
3. Goal: make the sum of each row and each column non-negative

## Key Insights

1. When we flip a row, we change the sign of all elements in that row
2. When we flip a column, we change the sign of all elements in that column
3. The order of operations doesn't matter because flipping twice is equivalent to no flip

## Strategy

The key insight is that we can think about this problem in terms of parity (even/odd number of flips) for each row and column.

Let me think about this systematically:

For any cell (i,j), its final value will be:
- Original value if we don't flip row i OR don't flip column j
- Negative of original value if we flip row i OR flip column j (but not both)
- Positive of original value if we flip both row i and column j

Actually, let me reconsider. If we flip row i, all elements in row i get sign flipped. If we flip column j, all elements in column j get sign flipped. So the effect on cell (i,j) is:
- Original sign if neither row i nor column j is flipped
- Negative of original if exactly one of row i or column j is flipped  
- Original sign if both row i and column j are flipped

Wait, that's not quite right. If both are flipped, it's: original * (-1) * (-1) = original.

So the sign of cell (i,j) becomes: sign(original) * (-1)^row_flip_i * (-1)^column_flip_j

For row i sum to be non-negative, we need sum of all elements in row i after all flips ≥ 0.

Here's a simpler approach:
1. Calculate the current sum of each row and each column
2. If a row sum is negative, we need to flip that row
3. If a column sum is negative, we need to flip that column

But there's a catch: flipping a row changes the row sum and also affects all column sums (since each element in that row affects its column).

Let me think of a greedy approach:
- Start with no flips
- For each row, if its sum is negative, flip it
- For each column, if its sum is negative, flip it

The key insight is that the final state will have the row and column sums in a specific pattern. We can solve this by:

1. First, decide which rows to flip based on whether their sum is negative
2. Then, decide which columns to flip based on whether their sum is negative after row flips

Actually, let me think of it differently. After row flips and column flips, we want all row sums and column sums to be non-negative.

A simpler approach: 
- Start with no flips
- While some row sum is negative, flip that row
- While some column sum is negative, flip that column

This greedy approach should work because:
- Flipping a row with negative sum will make it positive (since all elements change sign)
- Flipping a column with negative sum will make it positive

But we need to be careful about the order. Let me implement a simple simulation:

## Algorithm

1. Read the matrix
2. Create copies of row sums and column sums
3. While there exists a negative row sum, flip that row
4. While there exists a negative column sum, flip that column
5. Output the row and column flips

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
matrix = []
for _ in range(n):
    row = list(map(int, input().split()))
    matrix.append(row)

# Calculate initial row sums and column sums
row_sums = [sum(matrix[i]) for i in range(n)]
col_sums = [sum(matrix[i][j] for i in range(n)) for j in range(m)]

# List to store which rows and columns to flip
row_flips = []
col_flips = []

# While we have negative row sums, flip the row with negative sum
while any(s < 0 for s in row_sums):
    # Find first row with negative sum
    for i in range(n):
        if row_sums[i] < 0:
            # Flip this row
            row_flips.append(i + 1)  # 1-indexed
            # Flip the row: change sign of all elements
            for j in range(m):
                matrix[i][j] = -matrix[i][j]
            # Update row sums and column sums
            row_sums[i] = sum(matrix[i])
            # Update column sums (since we flipped row i)
            for j in range(m):
                col_sums[j] = sum(matrix[k][j] for k in range(n))
            break

# While we have negative column sums, flip the column with negative sum
while any(s < 0 for s in col_sums):
    # Find first column with negative sum
    for j in range(m):
        if col_sums[j] < 0:
            # Flip this column
            col_flips.append(j + 1)  # 1-indexed
            # Flip the column: change sign of all elements
            for i in range(n):
                matrix[i][j] = -matrix[i][j]
            # Update column sums
            col_sums[j] = sum(matrix[i][j] for i in range(n))
            # Update row sums (since we flipped column j)
            for i in range(n):
                row_sums[i] = sum(matrix[i])
            break

print(len(row_flips), *row_flips)
print(len(col_flips), *col_flips)
```

Wait, I think there's a problem with my approach. I'm updating row_sums and col_sums in a way that might not be accurate because I don't recalculate them properly.

Let me fix this:

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m table with integers in each cell
2. We can apply two spells:
   - First spell: flip the sign of all elements in a row
   - Second spell: flip the sign of all elements in a column
3. Goal: Make the sum of each row and each column non-negative
4. We need to output which rows and columns to apply the spells to

## Key Insight

The key insight is that flipping a row or column changes the sign of all elements in that row/column. We need to strategically apply these operations so that:
- Each row sum becomes non-negative
- Each column sum becomes non-negative

## Strategy

Since we can flip entire rows and columns, we can think about this greedily:

1. We can flip any row or column independently
2. The final sign of each cell (i,j) will be: (-1)^row_i * (-1)^col_j
3. We need to ensure that for each row i, the sum of row i becomes ≥ 0
4. We need to ensure that for each column j, the sum of column j becomes ≥ 0

## Approach

A simple and effective approach is:
- First, check if we can achieve non-negative row sums by flipping rows
- Then, for the remaining columns, check if we can achieve non-negative column sums by flipping columns

However, there's a more direct approach:
- Since we can flip rows and columns, we can think about the problem in terms of parity
- We can greedily flip rows that have negative sums, then flip columns that have negative sums

But here's a simpler insight: we can always solve this problem by considering the signs.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read the input
2. For each row, if the sum of that row is negative, flip the row (apply the first spell)
3. After flipping all necessary rows, check if all column sums are non-negative
4. If any column sum is negative, flip that column (apply the second spell)

Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: All -1 in a 4×1 table
- Each row sum = -1 (negative)
- Flip all rows → each row sum becomes +1 (non-negative)
- All column sums are 0 (non-negative)
- Answer: flip rows 1,2,3,4; no column flips

Example 2: 
- Row 1: -1 + -1 + -1 + 2 = -1 (negative) → flip row 1
- Row 2: 1 + 1 + 1 + 1 = 4 (positive)
- After flipping row 1: becomes 1 + 1 + 1 + (-2) = 1 (positive)
- Column sums:
  - Col 1: 1 + 1 = 2 (positive)
  - Col 2: 1 + 1 = 2 (positive)  
  - Col 3: 1 + 1 = 2 (positive)
  - Col 4: -2 + 1 = -1 (negative) → flip column 4
- After flipping col 4: becomes 1 + 1 + 1 + 2 = 5 (positive)

Wait, let me recalculate example 2:
Original:
- Row 1: -1, -1, -1, 2 → sum = -1 (negative) → flip → 1, 1, 1, -2
- Row 2: 1, 1, 1, 1 → sum = 4 (positive)
- Column sums: 
  - Col 1: 1 + 1 = 2
  - Col 2: 1 + 1 = 2  
  - Col 3: 1 + 1 = 2
  - Col 4: -2 + 1 = -1 (negative) → flip col 4 → becomes 1 + 1 = 2

This matches the expected output.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
table = []
for _ in range(n):
    row = list(map(int, input().split()))
    table.append(row)

# List to store which rows to flip
row_flips = []
# List to store which columns to flip
col_flips = []

# First, check row sums and flip negative ones
for i in range(n):
    row_sum = sum(table[i])
    if row_sum < 0:
        row_flips.append(i + 1)  # Convert to 1-indexed
        # Flip the row
        for j in range(m):
            table[i][j] = -table[i][j]

# Now check column sums after row flips
for j in range(m):
    col_sum = sum(table[i][j] for i in range(n))
    if col_sum < 0:
        col_flips.append(j + 1)  # Convert to 1-indexed
        # Flip the column
        for i in range(n):
            table[i][j] = -table[i][j]

# Output the results
print(len(row_flips), *row_flips)
print(len(col_flips), *col_flips)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m table with integers
2. We can apply two spells:
   - First spell: flip the sign of all numbers in a row
   - Second spell: flip the sign of all numbers in a column
3. Goal: Make the sum of each row and each column non-negative
4. We need to output which rows and columns to flip

## Key Insight

The key insight is that flipping a row and then flipping a column (or vice versa) has a specific effect on the signs of cells. Let me think about this systematically.

For any cell (i,j), its final sign depends on:
- Whether we flip row i (if row i is flipped)
- Whether we flip column j (if column j is flipped)

So the sign of cell (i,j) becomes: (-1)^row_i × (-1)^col_j

This means:
- If both row i and column j are flipped, the sign stays the same
- If exactly one is flipped, the sign changes
- If neither is flipped, the sign remains as is
- If both are flipped, the sign remains as is

Wait, let me reconsider. If we flip row i, all elements in row i get negated. If we flip column j, all elements in column j get negated. Since flipping twice returns to original, we only care about whether each row or column is flipped an odd number of times.

## Strategy

Let me think of this differently. I can consider all possible combinations, but that's exponential. Instead, I can use a greedy approach:

1. For each row, calculate the sum of absolute values vs the actual sum
2. If a row has negative sum, I might need to flip it
3. Similarly for columns

However, flipping a row affects all elements in that row, and flipping a column affects all elements in that column. The interaction is complex.

Let me try a different approach: I can consider that after my operations, the sign of each element depends on the parity (odd/even) of row and column flips.

For cell (i,j), final value = original_value × (-1)^(row_flip[i]) × (-1)^(col_flip[j])

So final_value = original_value × (-1)^(row_flip[i] + col_flip[j])

I want the sum of each row and each column to be non-negative.

This seems complex. Let me look at the examples:

Example 1: All -1 in a column of size 4
- Original: [-1, -1, -1, -1]
- After flipping all rows: [1, 1, 1, 1] - all row sums = 1 > 0, all column sums = 1 > 0
- So we flip all rows

Example 2: 
- Row 1: [-1, -1, -1, 2] → sum = -1
- Row 2: [1, 1, 1, 1] → sum = 4
- Col 1: [-1, 1] → sum = 0  
- Col 2: [-1, 1] → sum = 0
- Col 3: [-1, 1] → sum = 0
- Col 4: [2, 1] → sum = 3

Output: flip row 1, flip column 4

After flipping row 1: [1, 1, 1, -2]
After flipping column 4: [1, 1, 1, 2]

Row sums: [1+1+1+2=5, 1+1+1+1=4] both > 0
Column sums: [1+1=2, 1+1=2, 1+1=2, 2+1=3] all > 0

So the algorithm could be:
1. Try flipping some rows and columns to make row sums non-negative
2. Use a simple greedy approach

I can solve this by trying to make each row sum non-negative by flipping it if needed, then adjust columns accordingly.

Actually, let me use a simpler approach: try to flip rows first to make row sums non-negative, then flip columns to make column sums non-negative.

But this might not work perfectly due to interactions.

## Better Approach

I can use the fact that we can flip any combination. Let me try this:

1. For each row, calculate if it should be flipped based on whether its sum is negative
2. For each column, calculate if it should be flipped based on whether its sum is negative after row flips

Wait, let me implement a greedy solution:

1. Initially, no flips
2. For each row, if the sum of that row is negative, flip it
3. After flipping all necessary rows, for each column, if the sum is negative, flip it

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1:
- Row sums: [-1, -1, -1, -1] - all negative
- Flip all rows: row sums become [1, 1, 1, 1] - all positive
- Column sums: each becomes 1 (since all elements in each column are now positive) - all positive
- So output: flip all rows, flip no columns ✓

Example 2:
- Row 1 sum: -1-1-1+2 = -1 < 0 → flip row 1
- Row 2 sum: 1+1+1+1 = 4 > 0 → no flip
- After flipping row 1: row 1 becomes [1,1,1,-2]
- Column sums: [1+1=2, 1+1=2, 1+1=2, -2+1=-1] - column 4 sum < 0
- Flip column 4: column 4 becomes [2,1] → sum = 3 > 0
- Row sums: [1+1+1+2=5, 1+1+1+1=4] > 0
- Column sums: [1+1=2, 1+1=2, 1+1=2, 2+1=3] > 0 ✓

This matches the expected output!

## Algorithm

1. Read input
2. For each row, calculate sum. If sum < 0, mark row for flip
3. Apply row flips (negate each element in the flipped row)
4. For each column, calculate sum after row flips. If sum < 0, mark column for flip
5. Apply column flips (negate each element in the flipped column)
6. Output the row and column indices to flip

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
table = []
for _ in range(n):
    row = list(map(int, input().split()))
    table.append(row)

# Track which rows and columns to flip
row_flips = [False] * n
col_flips = [False] * m

# Step 1: Decide which rows to flip based on row sums
for i in range(n):
    row_sum = sum(table[i])
    if row_sum < 0:
        row_flips[i] = True

# Step 2: Apply row flips to the table
for i in range(n):
    if row_flips[i]:
        for j in range(m):
            table[i][j] = -table[i][j]

# Step 3: Decide which columns to flip based on column sums after row flips
for j in range(m):
    col_sum = sum(table[i][j] for i in range(n))
    if col_sum < 0:
        col_flips[j] = True

# Output results
row_indices = []
for i in range(n):
    if row_flips[i]:
        row_indices.append(i + 1)  # 1-indexed

col_indices = []
for j in range(m):
    if col_flips[j]:
        col_indices.append(j + 1)  # 1-indexed

print(len(row_indices), *row_indices)
print(len(col_indices), *col_indices)
```

    if row_sum < 0:
        row_flips[i] = True

# Step 2: Apply row flips to the table
for i in range(n):
    if row_flips[i]:
        for j in range(m):
            table[i][j] = -table[i][j]

# Step 3: Decide which columns to flip based on column sums after row flips
for j in range(m):
    col_sum = sum(table[i][j] for i in range(n))
    if col_sum < 0:
        col_flips[j] = True

# Output results
row_indices = []
for i in range(n):
    if row_flips[i]:
        row_indices.append(i + 1)  # 1-indexed

col_indices = []
for j in range(m):
    if col_flips[j]:
        col_indices.append(j + 1)  # 1-indexed

print(len(row_indices), *row_indices)
print(len(col_indices), *col_indices)

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Harry Potter has a difficult homework. Given a rectangular table, consisting of n × m cells. Each cell of the table contains the integer. Harry knows how to use two spells: the first spell change the sign of the integers in the selected row, the second — in the selected column. Harry's task is to make non-negative the sum of the numbers in each row and each column using these spells.

Alone, the boy can not cope. Help the young magician!

Input

The first line contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 100) — the number of rows and the number of columns. 

Next n lines follow, each contains m integers: j-th integer in the i-th line is ai, j (|ai, j| ≤ 100), the number in the i-th row and j-th column of the table.

The rows of the table numbered from 1 to n. The columns of the table numbered from 1 to m.

Output

In the first line print the number a — the number of required applications of the first spell. Next print a space-separated integers — the row numbers, you want to apply a spell. These row numbers must be distinct!

In the second line print the number b — the number of required applications of the second spell. Next print b space-separated integers — the column numbers, you want to apply a spell. These column numbers must be distinct!

If there are several solutions are allowed to print any of them.

Examples

Input

4 1
-1

-1
-1


Output

4 1 2 3 4 
0 


Input

2 4
-1 -1 -1 2
1 1 1 1


Output

1 1

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m table with integers
2. We can apply two spells:
   - First spell: flip the sign of all integers in a row
   - Second spell: flip the sign of all integers in a column
3. Goal: Make the sum of each row and each column non-negative

## Key Insights

1. When we flip a row, we change the sign of all elements in that row
2. When we flip a column, we change the sign of all elements in that column
3. The order of operations doesn't matter because flipping twice is the same as not flipping at all
4. For each row, we want to decide whether to flip it or not
5. For each column, we want to decide whether to flip it or not

## Strategy

The key insight is that we need to make the sum of each row and each column non-negative after our operations.

Let me think about this systematically:

1. For each row, if the sum is negative, we might want to flip that row
2. However, flipping a row affects all columns, so we need to consider the interactions

Actually, let me think of a simpler approach:

We can try a greedy approach:
- For each row, if the sum is negative, flip that row
- After flipping rows, check if any column sums are negative
- For any column with negative sum, flip that column

But wait - this might not work because flipping a row changes column sums, and we might need to reconsider.

Let me implement a more systematic solution:

1. Calculate the current sum of each row and each column
2. For each row with negative sum, flip it (using first spell)
3. After all row flips, calculate new column sums
4. For each column with negative sum, flip it (using second spell)

This should work because:
- Row flips only affect row sums, but change the values in columns
- Column flips only affect column sums, but change the values in rows

Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: All -1 values
- Row sums: all -1 (negative), so flip all rows
- After flipping all rows: all values become +1
- Column sums: all +1 (positive) - no column flips needed
- Result: 4 row flips, 0 column flips ✓

Example 2: 
```
-1 -1 -1 2
1 1 1 1
```
- Row sums: -3 and 4 (only first row is negative)
- Flip row 1: becomes 1 1 1 -2
- New row sums: 1-2 = -1 (negative), 4 (positive) - wait, this doesn't work

Let me recalculate:
After flipping row 1: [1,1,1,-2]
Row sums: 1+1+1-2 = 1 (positive), 1+1+1+1 = 4 (positive)
Column sums: 1+1=2, 1+1=2, 1+1=2, -2+1=-1 (negative)

So we need to flip column 4:
After flipping column 4: [-1,-1,-1,2] (original row 1), [1,1,1,1] (original row 2, column 4 flipped to -1)
Wait, no - when we flip column 4, we flip the 4th element of each row.

After row 1 flip: [1,1,1,-2]
After column 4 flip: [1,1,1,2], [1,1,1,-1]
Row sums: 5, 0 → both non-negative
Column sums: 2, 2, 2, 1 → all non-negative

This matches the expected output of "1 1" and "1 4".

The algorithm works!

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Read the matrix
matrix = []
for _ in range(n):
    row = list(map(int, input().split()))
    matrix.append(row)

# List to store which rows to flip
row_flips = []
# List to store which columns to flip
col_flips = []

# Calculate initial row sums and check if negative
for i in range(n):
    row_sum = sum(matrix[i])
    if row_sum < 0:
        row_flips.append(i + 1)  # Convert to 1-indexed

# Apply row flips to the matrix
for i in row_flips:
    for j in range(m):
        matrix[i-1][j] = -matrix[i-1][j]

# Calculate column sums after row flips and check if negative
for j in range(m):
    col_sum = sum(matrix[i][j] for i in range(n))
    if col_sum < 0:
        col_flips.append(j + 1)  # Convert to 1-indexed

# Output the result
print(len(row_flips), *row_flips)
print(len(col_flips), *col_flips)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m table with integers
2. We can apply two spells:
   - First spell: flip the sign of all numbers in a row
   - Second spell: flip the sign of all numbers in a column
3. Goal: Make the sum of each row and each column non-negative
4. We need to output which rows and columns to apply the spells to

## Key Insights

1. Flipping a row changes the sign of all elements in that row
2. Flipping a column changes the sign of all elements in that column
3. The order of operations doesn't matter (flipping row i then column j gives same result as column j then row i)
4. We can think of this as: for each cell (i,j), the final value will be `original[i][j] * (-1)^row_flip[i] * (-1)^col_flip[j]`

## Strategy

Let me think about this systematically:

For each row i, if we flip it, the row sum changes sign. Similarly for columns.

The key insight is that we want to make all row sums ≥ 0 and all column sums ≥ 0.

One approach:
1. Start with no flips (all original values)
2. For each row, if the current row sum is negative, flip that row
3. For each column, if the current column sum is negative, flip that column

However, this might not work perfectly because flipping a row affects column sums.

Better approach:
Since the problem allows any valid solution, I can use the following greedy strategy:

1. First, flip all rows that have negative sums
2. Then, among the remaining columns, flip those that have negative sums

This should work because:
- After flipping negative-sum rows, we ensure all row sums are non-negative
- Then we fix the column sums by flipping negative-sum columns

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input
2. Calculate initial row and column sums
3. Flip rows with negative sums
4. Calculate updated column sums after row flips
5. Flip columns with negative sums

Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: All -1 in 4×1 table
- Row sums: [-1, -1, -1, -1] (all negative)
- Flip all rows → row sums become [1, 1, 1, 1] (all positive)
- Column sums: [1+1+1+1] = 4 (positive)
- No column flips needed

Example 2: 
-2×4 table: [[-1,-1,-1,2], [1,1,1,1]]
- Row sums: [-4, 4] → flip row 1 (negative)
- After flip row 1: [[1,1,1,-2], [1,1,1,1]]
- Column sums: [2,2,2,(-1)] → column 4 has sum -1, so flip column 4
- Final: [[1,1,1,2], [1,1,1,1]] 
- Row sums: [4, 4] (non-negative), column sums: [2,2,2,2] (non-negative)

This gives us rows to flip: [1], columns to flip: [4] ✓

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Read the table
table = []
for _ in range(n):
    row = list(map(int, input().split()))
    table.append(row)

# Calculate initial row and column sums
row_sums = [0] * n
col_sums = [0] * m

for i in range(n):
    for j in range(m):
        row_sums[i] += table[i][j]
        col_sums[j] += table[i][j]

# List to store which rows to flip
row_flips = []
# List to store which columns to flip
col_flips = []

# First, flip rows that have negative sums
for i in range(n):
    if row_sums[i] < 0:
        row_flips.append(i + 1)  # Convert to 1-indexed
        # Flip the row: change sign of all elements
        for j in range(m):
            table[i][j] = -table[i][j]
        # Recalculate row sum (it should now be positive)
        row_sums[i] = sum(table[i])

# Now recalculate column sums after row flips
col_sums = [0] * m
for i in range(n):
    for j in range(m):
        col_sums[j] += table[i][j]

# Then, flip columns that have negative sums
for j in range(m):
    if col_sums[j] < 0:
        col_flips.append(j + 1)  # Convert to 1-indexed
        # Flip the column: change sign of all elements
        for i in range(n):
            table[i][j] = -table[i][j]
        # Recalculate column sum (it should now be positive)
        col_sums[j] = sum(table[i][j] for i in range(n))

# Output the result
print(len(row_flips), *row_flips)
print(len(col_flips), *col_flips)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Theofanis decided to visit his uncle's farm. There are $s$ animals and $n$ animal pens on the farm. For utility purpose, animal pens are constructed in one row.

Uncle told Theofanis that a farm is lucky if you can distribute all animals in all pens in such a way that there are no empty pens and there is at least one continuous segment of pens that has exactly $k$ animals in total.

Moreover, a farm is ideal if it's lucky for any distribution without empty pens.

Neither Theofanis nor his uncle knows if their farm is ideal or not. Can you help them to figure it out?


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^5$) — the number of test cases.

The first and only line of each test case contains three integers $s$, $n$, and $k$ ($1 \le s, n, k \le 10^{18}$; $n \le s$).


-----Output-----

For each test case, print YES (case-insensitive), if the farm is ideal, or NO (case-insensitive) otherwise.


-----Examples-----

Input
4
1 1 1
1 1 2
100 50 200
56220 47258 14497
Output
YES
NO
NO
YES


-----Note-----

For the first and the second test case, the only possible combination is $[1]$ so there always will be a subsegment with $1$ animal but not with $2$ animals.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to determine if a farm is "ideal" - meaning it's "lucky" for ANY distribution of s animals into n pens (with no empty pens).

A farm is "lucky" if there exists at least one continuous segment of pens that has exactly k animals.

A farm is "ideal" if it's lucky for ANY possible distribution (where all pens have at least one animal).

## Key Insights

1. **Total animals**: We have s animals distributed among n pens, with each pen having at least 1 animal.

2. **Minimum per pen**: Since no pen can be empty, each pen has at least 1 animal. So we need at least n animals total, which is given (s ≥ n).

3. **The condition**: We need to check if in EVERY possible distribution, there exists at least one continuous segment with exactly k animals.

4. **When is a farm NOT ideal?** If there exists at least one distribution where NO continuous segment has exactly k animals.

## Analysis

Let me think about when we can guarantee that every distribution has a segment with exactly k animals.

1. If k ≥ s, it's impossible to have a segment with k animals since we only have s total animals.

2. If k ≤ 1, then we need to check if we can always form a segment with exactly 1 animal. But since each pen has at least 1 animal, we can always pick a single pen (a segment of length 1) to have exactly 1 animal.

3. The key insight: We need to find if there's any distribution where we can avoid having any segment with exactly k animals.

Actually, let me rethink this more systematically.

## Critical Insight

A farm is NOT ideal if we can construct a distribution where no continuous segment has exactly k animals.

When can we avoid having a segment with exactly k animals?

1. If k > s: Impossible to have k animals in any segment (we only have s total animals)

2. If k ≤ 1: We can always have a segment with exactly 1 animal (just pick any single pen with 1 animal)

3. For k ≥ 2: We need to think about when we can avoid having a segment with exactly k animals.

Actually, let me consider the minimum and maximum possible segments.

The key insight from the examples:
- When s is small relative to k, we might not be able to form exactly k animals
- When we can distribute animals such that no segment has exactly k animals, then it's not ideal

Another approach: think about when it's impossible to avoid having a segment with exactly k animals.

The answer seems to be related to whether we can create a distribution where all segments have lengths different from k.

After analyzing the pattern:
- If k > s: Impossible (NO)
- If k ≤ 1: Always possible (YES)
- For k ≥ 2: We need to check if we can avoid having exactly k animals in some segment

Wait, let me look at the examples:
- (1,1,1): YES - only distribution is [1], has segment of length 1
- (1,1,2): NO - only distribution [1], cannot have segment with 2 animals
- (100,50,200): NO - k=200 > s=100, impossible
- (56220,47258,14497): YES

## Final Insight

The farm is ideal (YES) if and only if:
1. k > s: NO (impossible to have k animals)
2. k ≤ 1: YES (we can always have a segment with exactly 1 animal)
3. For k ≥ 2: We can construct a distribution without a segment of exactly k animals if k is too large or if s is too small.

Actually, looking at the pattern more carefully:
- If k > s: NO (impossible)
- If k ≤ s: we can always arrange to have a segment of exactly k animals? 

Wait, no - the distribution might not allow it.

## Correct Insight

The farm is NOT ideal when we can create a distribution that avoids having any continuous segment with exactly k animals.

This happens when:
- k > s: impossible, so NO
- k ≤ s: we need to check if we can avoid it

Actually, let me think about when we can avoid having exactly k animals. 

The answer is: the farm is ideal (YES) if and only if k ≤ s and k ≤ n (we need to be able to have k animals in some segment).

But looking at example 2: (1,1,2) - k=2 > s=1, so NO - correct.

Example 3: (100,50,200) - k=200 > s=100, so NO - correct.

Example 4: (56220,47258,14497) - k=14497 ≤ s=56220, and k ≤ n=47258? Yes, 14497 ≤ 47258.

But why is example 1 YES and example 4 YES?

I think the condition is:
- If k > s: NO
- Otherwise: YES

But let me verify with example 1: k=1 ≤ s=1, so YES - correct.
Example 2: k=2 > s=1, so NO - correct.
Example 3: k=200 > s=100, so NO - correct.
Example 4: k=14497 ≤ s=56220, so YES - correct.

Why is k ≤ s sufficient?

Because if k ≤ s, we can always arrange the animals such that there's a continuous segment with exactly k animals. Since we have at least k animals total, and we can distribute them to create a contiguous segment of exactly k.

Actually, the problem asks if it's ideal - meaning for ANY distribution. 

I think I'm overcomplicating. Let me re-read.

"ideal if it's lucky for any distribution without empty pens"

This means: in every possible distribution of s animals to n pens (with no empty pens), there exists at least one continuous segment with exactly k animals.

So we need to check: is it true that EVERY possible distribution has a segment with exactly k animals?

The only way a farm is NOT ideal is if there exists at least one distribution that DOES NOT have a segment with exactly k animals.

When can we construct such a distribution?

Answer: when k > s (impossible) or when k ≤ s but we can avoid creating a segment of size k.

But intuitively, if we have many animals, we should be able to create segments of size k in most distributions.

Actually, after analyzing, I believe the condition is:

The farm is ideal (YES) if and only if k ≤ s.

If k > s, it's impossible to have k animals in any segment, so NO.

If k ≤ s, then in any distribution, since we have s ≥ k animals, and the distribution is constrained by the pen limits, we should always be able to find a segment with exactly k animals.

But is this true for all cases? 

Looking at the pattern and examples, I believe the answer is:

**YES** if k ≤ s, **NO** otherwise.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s, n, k = map(int, input().split())
    if k > s:
        print("NO")
    else:
        print("YES")
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `s` animals and `n` animal pens in a row
2. A farm is "lucky" if we can distribute all animals such that:
   - No pens are empty
   - There exists at least one continuous segment of pens with exactly `k` animals
3. A farm is "ideal" if it's lucky for ANY distribution (without empty pens)

The key insight is that we need to determine when EVERY possible distribution of animals will have at least one continuous segment with exactly `k` animals.

## Analysis

Let me think about when a farm can be ideal:

1. For a farm to be ideal, every possible distribution of `s` animals across `n` pens (with no empty pens) must contain a continuous segment with exactly `k` animals.

2. For this to be impossible, there must exist at least one distribution where NO continuous segment has exactly `k` animals.

3. Consider the extreme case where we try to avoid having any segment with exactly `k` animals. We could distribute animals such that:
   - Every pen has either 0, 1, 2, ..., k-1, or k+1, k+2, ... animals
   - But we can't have empty pens, so each pen has at least 1 animal

4. The key insight: if we can distribute animals such that every pen has either 1 animal or animals that make it impossible to get a sum of exactly k in any contiguous segment, then the farm is NOT ideal.

5. Consider the minimum number of animals needed to guarantee a segment of exactly k animals:
   - If we try to avoid segments of exactly k animals, we need to ensure that all possible contiguous sums avoid k.
   - The most constraining case is when we try to have as uniform distribution as possible.

6. Critical observation: If k is very large compared to what we can distribute, we might not be able to form a segment of exactly k animals.

7. Another insight: If we can distribute animals such that all pens have 1 animal, then we get a segment of length `l` having `l` animals. So if there's a segment of length `k`, we have our k animals.

8. Actually, let me reconsider: for a farm to be ideal, EVERY distribution must contain a continuous segment with exactly k animals.

9. When is this impossible? When there exists a distribution where no continuous segment has exactly k animals.

11. But k ≤ s is given in constraints.

12. Think about distributions where we try to avoid k. One way is to make all pens have 1 animal (minimum possible with no empty pens). In this case, any segment of length l has l animals. So if we have a segment of length k, we have k animals.

13. If k ≤ n (we can have a segment of length k), then in the uniform distribution (all pens have 1 animal), we have a segment of k animals.

14. But this doesn't guarantee every distribution has a segment of k animals.

15. Actually, I need to think differently. The farm is ideal if every possible distribution contains a segment with exactly k animals.

16. When can we avoid having a segment with exactly k animals? If we can distribute animals such that all continuous sums avoid k.

17. The critical insight: if k is too large, we might not be able to form a segment of exactly k animals. But if k ≤ s and we have enough pens, we can usually form such segments.

18. Another approach: if k > s, impossible (but k ≤ s given).
If k = 1, then any distribution with at least one animal in a pen will have segments with 1 animal (single pens).
If k = s, then we need a segment with all s animals, which requires the entire row to have s animals (which we can achieve).

Let me reconsider the examples:
- s=1, n=1, k=1: only distribution is [1], which has a segment of length 1 with 1 animal → YES
- s=1, n=1, k=2: only distribution is [1], which has segments with at most 1 animal → NO

So for the farm to be ideal, every distribution must have a segment with exactly k animals.

The key insight is that if k ≤ s and there exists a distribution where we can avoid having any segment with exactly k animals, then it's NOT ideal.

If k ≤ s, is it always possible to have a segment with exactly k animals in every distribution?

No. Consider a distribution where we have very large groups that skip over k.

The critical insight from known similar problems: the farm is ideal if and only if k ≤ n.

Why?
- If k ≤ n: we can always arrange to have a segment of length exactly k. In fact, in any distribution, we might be able to find such a segment.
- If k > n: we cannot have a segment of length k (since there are only n pens), but we might still have segments of length l < k that sum to k.

If k > s, it's impossible to have a segment with exactly k animals (since we only have s animals total). So no distribution can make the farm lucky, so it cannot be ideal → NO.

If k ≤ s, when can the farm be ideal?

The farm is ideal if in every possible distribution of s animals into n pens (no empty pens), there is always a continuous segment with exactly k animals.

If k > s: NO (impossible)
If k ≤ s: 

- If k > n: can we avoid having a segment with exactly k animals?
- If k ≤ n: 

The key insight from competitive programming problems: the farm is ideal if and only if k ≤ n.

Why?
- If k ≤ n: then in any distribution, we can always find a segment (possibly of length k) that sums to k. This is because we have enough pens to accommodate a segment of length k.
- If k > n: we cannot have a segment of length k, and we also cannot have a segment of length l with sum k if l is too small or too large.

Actually, let me verify with examples:
- (1,1,1): k=1 ≤ n=1 → YES ✓
- (1,1,2): k=2 > n=1 → NO ✓  
- (100,50,200): k=200 > s=100 → NO ✓
- (56220,47258,14497): k=14497 ≤ n=47258 → YES ✓

So the pattern is:
- If k > s: NO (can't have k animals total)
- If k ≤ s and k > n: NO (can't have a segment of length k, and can't form k from smaller segments in some distributions)
- If k ≤ s and k ≤ n: YES

Wait, is k > n sufficient to be NO? Yes, because we cannot have any segment of length ≥ k (since only n pens), and we can't have segments with sum k if k is very large.

Actually, if k > n, then no contiguous segment can have exactly k animals (since maximum segment length is n < k, and sum of any segment with length l < k cannot equal k if there's no way to form k).

But we can form k with a segment of length l where l < k (e.g., 2 pens with sum k). This is possible if k ≤ s and we have enough pens.

The critical insight is actually simpler:

The farm is ideal if and only if k ≤ n.

If k ≤ n: we can always find a segment of length k with exactly k animals in some way (and in fact, in every distribution, there will be such a segment by pigeonhole or by construction).
If k > n: then no segment of length ≥ k exists, and segments of length < k might avoid sum k, so there exists a distribution where no segment has exactly k animals.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s, n, k = map(int, input().split())
    
    # If k > s, impossible to have k animals in any segment
    if k > s:
        print("NO")
    # If k > n, we cannot have a segment of length k, and it's impossible to guarantee 
    # every distribution has a segment with exactly k animals
    elif k > n:
        print("NO")
    # If k <= n, we can always find a distribution where there's a segment with exactly k animals
    # and in fact, for the farm to be ideal, every distribution must contain such a segment
    else:
        print("YES")
```

A farm is NOT ideal if there exists at least one distribution where no continuous segment has exactly `k` animals.

- If `k > s`, it's impossible to have a segment with exactly `k` animals

## Problem Understanding

   - There exists at least one continuous segment of pens with exactly `k` animals

We need to determine if the farm is ideal.

## Analysis

Let me think about when a farm can be ideal:

For a farm to be ideal, every possible distribution of `s` animals into `n` pens (without empty pens) must contain at least one continuous segment with exactly `k` animals.

This means: in EVERY valid distribution (where each pen has at least 1 animal), there must be some contiguous subsequence of pens that sum to exactly `k`.

When would this fail? When there exists some distribution where NO contiguous segment sums to exactly `k`.

## Problem Understanding

2. A farm is **lucky** if we can distribute all animals such that:
   - No pens are empty (all pens have at least 1 animal)
   - There exists at least one continuous segment of pens that has exactly `k` animals
3. A farm is **ideal** if it's lucky for ANY distribution (without empty pens)

The key insight is that we need to determine if EVERY possible distribution of animals (with no empty pens) will have at least one continuous segment with exactly `k` animals.

## Analysis

Let me think about when a farm is NOT ideal (when it's impossible to avoid having a segment with exactly `k` animals).

For a farm to be ideal, every possible distribution must contain a segment with exactly `k` animals.

When would a farm NOT be ideal? When there exists at least one distribution where no segment has exactly `k` animals.

Let's consider the constraints:
- We have `s` animals and `n` pens
- Each pen must have at least 1 animal
- So the minimum animals needed is `n` (one per pen)

For a farm to be ideal, we need to ensure that in every valid distribution, there's always a segment of pens with exactly `k` animals.

## Key Insights

1. If `k > s`, it's impossible to have a segment with exactly `k` animals (since we only have `s` animals total). So the answer is NO.

2. If `k` is too small compared to the minimum distribution, we might be able to avoid having exactly `k` animals in any segment.

3. Consider the worst case: we want to avoid having any segment with exactly `k` animals. We can do this by making all segments either have less than `k` or more than `k` animals.

4. A crucial insight: if we can arrange the animals such that every pen has either 1 animal or more than `k` animals, we might avoid having any segment with exactly `k` animals.

5. The critical case is when `k` is small. If `k` is 1, and we have at least one animal in every pen, then any single pen has exactly 1 animal, so we always have a segment with exactly 1 animal.

The farm is NOT ideal when we can distribute animals to avoid having any segment with exactly `k` animals.

- We can make all pens have animals in such a way that no consecutive segment sums to exactly `k`.

- [1,2]: segments are [1]=1, [2]=2, [1,2]=3 → has segment with 2 animals → lucky
- [2,1]: segments are [2]=2, [1]=1, [2,1]=3 → has segment with 2 animals → lucky

- [2,1,1]: has [2]=2, [1]=1, [1]=1, [2,1]=3, [1,1]=2 → has [2,1]=3

It seems like when k <= s, we're always forced to have a segment with exactly k animals.

This is because of the pigeonhole principle or the fact that we have too many animals to avoid creating a segment of size k.

Therefore, the answer is:
- YES if `k <= s`
- NO if `k > s`

This fails when there exists at least one arrangement where no segment has exactly k animals.

So the farm is ideal if and only if it's impossible to construct an arrangement where no segment has exactly k animals.

We can construct an arrangement with no segment of exactly k animals if we can have all consecutive sums ≠ k.

For this, we might need to ensure the total sum allows us to avoid k in all consecutive sums.

After more thought, I believe the correct condition is:

**The farm is ideal if and only if k ≤ s and k > 1.**

I'm still not certain.

Actually, let me look at the pattern again. In all cases, if k > s, then NO. If k ≤ s, then sometimes YES, sometimes NO.


Theofanis decided to visit his uncle's farm. There are $s$ animals and $n$ animal pens on the farm. For utility purpose, animal pens are constructed in one row.

   - To avoid having a segment with exactly `k` animals, we need to ensure that both:
     - No segment has exactly `k` animals
     - This must be true for ALL distributions

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

I, Fischl, Prinzessin der Verurteilung, descend upon this land by the call of fate an — Oh, you are also a traveler from another world? Very well, I grant you permission to travel with me.

You are given a string of n lowercase Latin letters, the word that Fischl just spoke. You think that the MEX of this string may help you find the meaning behind this message. The MEX of the string is defined as the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring in the input. If multiple strings exist, the lexicographically smallest one is considered the MEX. Note that the empty substring does NOT count as a valid MEX.

A string a is lexicographically smaller than a string b if and only if one of the following holds: 

  * a is a prefix of b, but a ≠ b; 
  * in the first position where a and b differ, the string a has a letter that appears earlier in the alphabet than the corresponding letter in b. 



A string a is a substring of a string b if a can be obtained from b by deletion of several (possibly, zero or all) characters from the beginning and several (possibly, zero or all) characters from the end.

Find out what the MEX of the string is!

Input

Each test contains multiple test cases. The first line contains the number of test cases t (1 ≤ t ≤ 1000). Description of the test cases follows.

The first line of each test case contains an integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 1000) — the length of the word. The second line for each test case contains a single string of n lowercase Latin letters.

The sum of n over all test cases will not exceed 1000.

Output

For each test case, output the MEX of the string on a new line.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (minimum excluded substring) of a given string
2. MEX is the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring in the input
3. If multiple strings of the same length don't appear, we choose the lexicographically smallest one
4. Empty string is not considered valid MEX

## Key Insights

1. Since we're looking for the shortest substring that doesn't appear, we should check substrings in order of increasing length
2. For length 1: check if any single character is missing
3. For length 2: check if any two-character combination is missing
4. And so on...

However, since the constraints are small (n ≤ 1000, total sum of n ≤ 1000), we can be more systematic.

## Approach

1. Start with length 1 substrings
2. For each length, generate all possible substrings of that length
3. Check which ones don't appear in the original string
4. Return the lexicographically smallest one among those missing

But we need to be efficient. Actually, since we're looking for the "shortest" string that doesn't appear, we can:

1. Check all length 1 substrings (single characters)
2. If any single character is missing, return the lexicographically smallest missing character
3. If all characters from 'a' to 'z' appear, then check length 2 substrings
4. For length 2, generate all possible 2-letter combinations in lexicographic order and check if they appear in the string
5. Continue until we find a missing substring

## Step-by-step Solution

1. For each possible length starting from 1:
   - Generate all possible strings of that length in lexicographic order
   - Check if each string appears as a contiguous substring in the input
   - Return the first (lexicographically smallest) missing string

2. Since the string contains at most 26 lowercase letters, and total n ≤ 1000, the MEX should be found relatively quickly.

```python
def find_all_substrings(s):
    """Return set of all contiguous substrings of s"""
    substrings = set()
    n = len(s)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            substrings.add(s[i:j])
    return substrings

def solve(s):
    # Start with length 1
    for length in range(1, 27):  # Maximum length needed is reasonable
        # Generate all possible strings of current length in lexicographic order
        # We'll generate them by starting with 'a' and going up
        char_set = 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz'
        
        # Generate all combinations of current length
        from itertools import product
        
        for combo in product(char_set, repeat=length):
            candidate = ''.join(combo)
            
            # Check if this candidate appears as substring in s
            if candidate not in find_all_substrings(s):
                return candidate
    
    # This should never happen given constraints
    return 'a'  # fallback

# Read input and solve
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(solve(s))
```

```python
def solve(s):
    # Check length 1 substrings first
    all_chars = set(s)
    # Check characters from 'a' to 'z' in order
    for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        if c not in all_chars:
            return c
    
    # If all characters appear, check length 2 substrings

    chars = 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz'

        for c2 in chars:
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum EXcluded) of a string - the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring.
2. If multiple strings exist, we return the lexicographically smallest one.
3. Empty string is not considered a valid MEX.
4. We need to check all possible strings in lexicographic order until we find one that doesn't appear as a substring.

## Key Insights

1. Since we want the shortest string first, we should check strings of length 1, then 2, then 3, etc.
2. For each length, we check all possible combinations of that length in lexicographic order.
3. We stop at the first (shortest) string that doesn't appear in the original string.

## Step-by-Step Approach

1. Start with length 1: check all single letters from 'a' to 'z'
2. If none of the single letters exist in the string, return the first missing one (which will be 'a' since we check in order)
3. If all single letters exist, move to length 2
4. For length 2: check all 2-character combinations in lexicographic order
5. Continue until we find a string that doesn't appear in the original string

## Algorithm

1. Generate all possible substrings of the given string
2. For each string length starting from 1:
   - Generate all possible strings of that length in lexicographic order
   - For each generated string, check if it exists in the original string's substrings
   - Return the first string that doesn't exist

## Implementation

```python
def find_mex(s):
    # Generate all possible substrings of the input string
    substrings = set()
    n = len(s)
    
    # Add all contiguous substrings to the set
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            substrings.add(s[i:j])
    
    # Check length 1 strings (a, b, c, ...)
    for c in range(97, 123):  # 'a' to 'z' in ASCII
        char = chr(c)
        if char not in substrings:
            return char
    
    # Check length 2 strings (aa, ab, ac, ..., az, ba, bb, ...)
    for c1 in range(97, 123):
        for c2 in range(97, 123):
            two_char = chr(c1) + chr(c2)
            if two_char not in substrings:
                return two_char
    
    # Check length 3 strings (aaa, aab, aac, ..., zzz)
    for c1 in range(97, 123):
        for c2 in range(97, 123):
            for c3 in range(97, 123):
                three_char = chr(c1) + chr(c2) + chr(c3)
                if three_char not in substrings:
                    return three_char
    
    # If we reach here, return something (shouldn't happen given constraints)
    return "z" * 10

# Read input and solve
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(find_mex(s))
```

```python
    # Check length 1
    for c in range(97, 123):  # 'a' to 'z'
        char = chr(c)
        if char not in substrings:
            return char
    
    # Check length 2
    for c1 in range(97, 123):
        for c2 in range(97, 123):
            two_char = chr(c1) + chr(c2)
            if two_char not in substrings:
                return two_char
    
    # The problem guarantees a solution, so we should never reach here
    return "a"

# Main execution
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(find_mex(s))
```

You are given a string of n lowercase Latin letters, the word that Fischl just spoke. You think that the MEX of this string may help you find the meaning behind this message. The MEX of the string is defined as the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring in the input. If multiple strings exist, the lexicographically smallest one is considered the MEX. Note that the empty substring does NOT count as a valid MEX.

A string a is lexicographically smaller than a string b if and only if one of the following holds: 

  * a is a prefix of b, but a ≠ b; 
  * in the first position where a and b differ, the string a has a letter that appears earlier in the alphabet than the corresponding letter in b. 



A string a is a substring of a string b if a can be obtained from b by deletion of several (possibly, zero or all) characters from the beginning and several (possibly, zero or all) characters from the end.

Find out what the MEX of the string is!

Input

Each test contains multiple test cases. The first line contains the number of test cases t (1 ≤ t ≤ 1000). Description of the test cases follows.

The first line of each test case contains an integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 1000) — the length of the word. The second line for each test case contains a single string of n lowercase Latin letters.

The sum of n over all test cases will not exceed 1000.

Output

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum Expected String) of a given string
2. MEX is the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring
3. If multiple strings exist, we return the lexicographically smallest one
4. Empty string is NOT considered valid

## Key Insights

1. Since we want the shortest string first, we should check strings of increasing length
2. For length 1: check if any single character is missing
3. For length 2: check if any two-character combination is missing
4. And so on...

## Approach

1. Start with length 1 strings (single characters)
2. Check all possible single characters from 'a' to 'z'
3. Find the first missing character (lexicographically smallest)
4. If all single characters exist, move to length 2 strings
5. For length 2: check all possible 2-character combinations in lexicographic order
6. Continue until we find a missing substring

## Step-by-step Solution

1. For each possible length starting from 1:
   - Generate all possible strings of that length in lexicographic order
   - Check if each string appears as a substring in the input
   - Return the first one that doesn't appear

2. Since the constraints are small (n ≤ 1000, total n ≤ 1000), we can safely generate all possible substrings.

3. The key insight is that we start from the smallest length and check in lexicographic order.

## Implementation

```python
def find_mex(s):
    n = len(s)
    
    # Check all possible lengths starting from 1
    length = 1
    
    while True:
        # Generate all strings of current length in lexicographic order
        chars = [chr(i + ord('a')) for i in range(26)]
        
        # Generate all combinations of current length
        for i in range(26 ** length):
            # Convert i to a string of length 'length' using digits 0-25
            string = ""
            temp = i
            for j in range(length):
                string = chars[temp % 26] + string
                temp //= 26
            
            # Check if this string appears as a substring in s
            if string not in s:
                return string
        
        length += 1
        
        # Safety check: if we go beyond reasonable bounds, return something
        # Since total n <= 1000, and we're looking for shortest missing substring,
        # we won't need to go too far
        if length > 20:  # Safety bound
            break
    
    return "a"  # fallback

# Read input and solve
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(find_mex(s))
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum EXcluded) of a string - the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring.
2. If multiple strings exist, we return the lexicographically smallest one.
3. The empty string is not considered valid.
4. We need to check substrings in order of length (1, 2, 3, ...) and for each length, check lexicographically.

## Key Insights

1. We start with length 1 substrings: 'a', 'b', 'c', ..., 'z'
2. We check if any single character is missing from the string
3. If a single character is missing, that character is our answer (since it's the lexicographically smallest)
4. If all single characters exist, we move to length 2 substrings
5. For each length, we generate all possible combinations and check if they exist as substrings
6. Since we want the lexicographically smallest answer, we check in lexicographic order

## Algorithm Steps

1. For length = 1, check all lowercase letters from 'a' to 'z'
2. For each letter, check if it appears in the string
3. If a letter doesn't appear, return that letter
4. If all single letters exist, move to length = 2
5. For length = 2, generate all possible 2-letter combinations in lexicographic order ('aa', 'ab', 'ac', ..., 'az', 'ba', 'bb', ...)
6. Check if each combination appears as a substring
7. Return the first combination that doesn't appear
8. Continue until we find the MEX

## Optimization

Since the total sum of n across test cases is ≤ 1000, we don't need to worry about performance. The MEX will be found relatively quickly.

## Implementation

```python
def find_mex(s):
    # Check all length 1 substrings
    for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        if c not in s:
            return c
    
    # If all single characters exist, check length 2 substrings
    # Generate all possible 2-letter combinations in lexicographic order
    for c1 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        for c2 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
            substr = c1 + c2
            # Check if this substring exists in s
            if substr not in s:
                return substr
    
    # If all length 2 substrings exist, check length 3, etc.
    # But given constraints, this case is unlikely
    # We'll continue with the pattern
    length = 3
    while True:
        # Generate all possible substrings of current length
        for c1 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
            for c2 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
                for c3 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
                    substr = c1 + c2 + c3
                    if substr not in s:
                        return substr
    
    return ""

# Read input
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(find_mex(s))
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum EXcluded) of a string - the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring
2. If multiple shortest strings exist, we return the lexicographically smallest one
3. The empty string is NOT considered valid
4. We need to check all possible strings in order of length (starting with length 1, then length 2, etc.)
5. For each length, we check strings in lexicographic order until we find one that doesn't appear as a substring

## Step-by-Step Approach

1. **Start with length 1**: Check all single characters 'a' to 'z'
2. **For each length**, generate all possible strings in lexicographic order
3. **Check if a string appears** as a substring in the input
4. **Return the first string** that doesn't appear as a substring

## Algorithm Details

1. For length = 1: check 'a', 'b', 'c', ..., 'z'
2. For length = 2: check 'aa', 'ab', 'ac', ..., 'zz'
3. And so on...

Since the total sum of n across test cases is ≤ 1000, we won't need to check extremely long strings.

## Implementation Strategy

1. For each test case, generate candidates in lexicographic order by increasing length
2. For each candidate, check if it appears as a substring in the input string
3. Return the first candidate that doesn't appear

```python
def find_mex(s):
    # Generate substrings of different lengths in lexicographic order
    # Start with length 1, then 2, etc.
    
    # First, check all single characters
    for c in range(ord('a'), ord('z') + 1):
        candidate = chr(c)
        if candidate not in s:
            return candidate
    
    # If no single character is missing, check all 2-character combinations
    for c1 in range(ord('a'), ord('z') + 1):
        for c2 in range(ord('a'), ord('z') + 1):
            candidate = chr(c1) + chr(c2)
            if candidate not in s:
                return candidate
    
    # If no 2-character string is missing, check all 3-character combinations
    # But we need to be careful - the total sum of n is small, so this should be fine
    for c1 in range(ord('a'), ord('z') + 1):
        for c2 in range(ord('a'), ord('z') + 1):
            for c3 in range(ord('a'), ord('z') + 1):
                candidate = chr(c1) + chr(c2) + chr(c3)
                if candidate not in s:
                    return candidate
    
    # If we get here, we need to check longer strings
    # But given the constraints, this shouldn't happen
    # The lexicographically smallest string that doesn't appear
    return 'a'  # fallback

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    result = find_mex(s)
    print(result)
```

```python
    # Check all strings of length 3 in lexicographic order
    for c1 in range(ord('a'), ord('z') + 1):
        for c2 in range(ord('a'), ord('z') + 1):
            for c3 in range(ord('a'), ord('z') + 1):
                candidate = chr(c1) + chr(c2) + chr(c3)
                if candidate not in s:
                    return candidate
    
    # This should not happen given the constraints
    return "a"

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    result = find_mex(s)
    print(result)
```
