You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

I, Fischl, Prinzessin der Verurteilung, descend upon this land by the call of fate an — Oh, you are also a traveler from another world? Very well, I grant you permission to travel with me.

It is no surprise Fischl speaks with a strange choice of words. However, this time, not even Oz, her raven friend, can interpret her expressions! Maybe you can help us understand what this young princess is saying?

You are given a string of n lowercase Latin letters, the word that Fischl just spoke. You think that the MEX of this string may help you find the meaning behind this message. The MEX of the string is defined as the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring in the input. If multiple strings exist, the lexicographically smallest one is considered the MEX. Note that the empty substring does NOT count as a valid MEX.

A string a is lexicographically smaller than a string b if and only if one of the following holds: 

  * a is a prefix of b, but a ≠ b; 
  * in the first position where a and b differ, the string a has a letter that appears earlier in the alphabet than the corresponding letter in b. 



A string a is a substring of a string b if a can be obtained from b by deletion of several (possibly, zero or all) characters from the beginning and several (possibly, zero or all) characters from the end.

Find out what the MEX of the string is!

Input

Each test contains multiple test cases. The first line contains the number of test cases t (1 ≤ t ≤ 1000). Description of the test cases follows.

The first line of each test case contains an integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 1000) — the length of the word. The second line for each test case contains a single string of n lowercase Latin letters.

The sum of n over all test cases will not exceed 1000.

Output

For each test case, output the MEX of the string on a new line.

Example

Input


3
28
qaabzwsxedcrfvtgbyhnujmiklop
13
cleanairactbd
10
aannttoonn


Output


ac
f
b

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum Excluded) of a string - the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring.
2. If multiple strings exist, we take the lexicographically smallest one.
3. The empty string doesn't count as valid MEX.
4. We're looking for the shortest string that isn't present as a substring.

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Understand what MEX means
- We need to check all possible strings in lexicographic order starting from length 1.
- For each length, we check all possible combinations of that length.
- The first (shortest) string that doesn't appear as a substring is our answer.

### Step 2: Algorithm approach
1. Start with length 1 strings: "a", "b", "c", ..., "z"
2. For each length, generate all possible strings in lexicographic order
3. Check if each string appears as a substring in the input
4. Return the first string that doesn't appear

### Step 3: Optimization considerations
- Since the total sum of n across test cases is only 1000, we don't need to optimize heavily
- We can generate strings in lexicographic order and check existence in the input string
- We need a function to check if a string exists as a substring

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
def find_mex(s):
    # Check all possible strings in lexicographic order
    # Start with length 1, then length 2, etc.
    
    # For length 1
    for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        if c not in s:
            return c
    
    # For length 2
    for c1 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        for c2 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
            substr = c1 + c2
            if substr not in s:
                return substr
    
    # For length 3
    for c1 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        for c2 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
            for c3 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
                substr = c1 + c2 + c3
                if substr not in s:
                    return substr
    
    # If we reach here, it means all strings of length 1, 2, 3 exist
    # But since the problem guarantees a solution exists, this shouldn't happen
    return "a"  # fallback

# Read input
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(find_mex(s))
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum Excluded) of a string - the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring
2. If multiple such strings exist, we return the lexicographically smallest one
3. The empty string is NOT considered valid
4. We need to check all possible strings in lexicographic order until we find one that doesn't appear as a substring

## Approach Analysis

Since we want the shortest string first, and then lexicographically smallest, we should:

1. Check all strings of length 1 (a, b, c, ..., z)
2. If none of them appear as substrings, return the first one
3. If some of them appear, check all strings of length 2 (aa, ab, ac, ..., az, ba, bb, ...)
4. And so on...

But we need to be smart about this. Since we want the lexicographically smallest non-existent string, we should:

1. First check all single characters (a to z)
2. For each character, check if it appears as a substring
3. If a character doesn't appear, return that character
4. If all characters appear, check all 2-character combinations (aa, ab, ac, ... az, ba, bb, ...), and return the first one that doesn't appear
5. Continue this process until we find a string that doesn't appear

## Optimization Considerations

- The constraint says the total n over all test cases ≤ 1000, so we don't need to optimize too much
- We only need to check a reasonable number of strings before finding the answer
- Since the alphabet has only 26 letters, for length 1 we have at most 26 possibilities
- For length 2, we have at most 26×26 = 676 possibilities

## Algorithm Steps

1. For length = 1: check each letter from 'a' to 'z'
2. For each letter that doesn't appear in the string, return it
3. If all single letters appear, go to length = 2
4. For length = 2: check each 2-letter combination in lexicographic order
5. Return the first combination that doesn't appear as a substring
6. Continue until we find a valid MEX

## Implementation

```python
def solve(s):
    n = len(s)
    
    # Check all strings of length 1
    for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        if c not in s:
            return c
    
    # Check all strings of length 2
    for c1 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        for c2 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
            substring = c1 + c2
            if substring not in s:
                return substring
    
    # Check all strings of length 3
    for c1 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        for c2 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
            for c3 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
                substring = c1 + c2 + c3
                if substring not in s:
                    return substring
    
    # For very long strings or edge cases, this should theoretically find something
    # But given the constraints, we should always find it within length 3
    # In practice, the MEX is usually very short
    
    # If we get here, return the first 3-letter combination (shouldn't happen)
    return 'abc'

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(solve(s))
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum Excluded) of a string - the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring
2. If there are multiple shortest strings that don't appear, we choose the lexicographically smallest one
3. Empty string is not considered valid MEX
4. We need to check all possible strings in order of length, and for each length, check lexicographically

## Step-by-Step Approach

1. **Start with shortest possible strings**: We check strings of length 1, then length 2, etc.
2. **For each length**, generate all possible strings in lexicographic order
3. **Check if each string appears** as a substring in the input
4. **Return the first string** that doesn't appear

## Key Insights

- Strings of length 1: 'a', 'b', 'c', ... 'z'
- If any character from 'a' to 'z' doesn't appear in the input, that character is our answer
- If all characters a-z appear, we move to length 2 strings
- For length 2: 'aa', 'ab', 'ac', ..., 'az', 'ba', 'bb', ... 
- We need to generate all possible combinations in lexicographic order

## Algorithm

1. Start with length = 1
2. Generate all strings of current length in lexicographic order
3. For each string, check if it's a substring of the input
4. Return the first string that is not found

## Implementation Strategy

Since the total sum of n over all test cases is ≤ 1000, and the string length is small (≤ 1000), we don't need to be overly efficient.

```python
def find_mex(s):
    # Check all strings of length 1
    for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        if c not in s:
            return c

        for c2 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
            substring = c1 + c2
            if substring not in s:
                return substring

    # However, we should handle the case where all length 1 and 2 strings appear
```

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7



-----Note-----

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. For these two stations the distance is $2$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

2. After adding these edges, we need to find the sum of all pairwise distances

**Example 1:** Star graph with center 1 connected to 2, 3, 4
- Original: 1-2, 1-3, 1-4
- Any two nodes share a common neighbor (all share neighbor 1)

- Distances: (1,2)=1, (1,3)=1, (1,4)=1, (2,3)=1, (2,4)=1, (3,4)=1
- Sum = 6

**Example 2:** Path 1-2-3-4
- Original edges: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4
- Nodes that share common neighbors:
  - 1 and 3: share neighbor 2
  - 2 and 4: share neighbor 3
  - 1 and 4: no common neighbor (neighbors are 2 and 3 respectively)

So adjacent nodes in original tree don't get a direct edge in the new graph? But they should because they are adjacent.

I think I made a mistake. The operation is: add edges between any two stations that share a common neighbor. It doesn't add edges between adjacent stations.

So adjacent stations in original don't automatically get a new edge. The new edges are only between stations that share a common neighbor.

So in original tree:
- Nodes are adjacent → distance 1
- Nodes that share a common neighbor → distance 1 in new graph
- All other pairs → distance 2 in new graph?

But in example 2: 
- Original path: 1-2-3-4
- Nodes that share a common neighbor:
  - 1 and 3: share neighbor 2 → get edge (1,3)
  - 2 and 4: share neighbor 3 → get edge (2,4)
- Nodes 1 and 2: were adjacent → distance = 1
- Nodes 1 and 3: get edge → distance = 1  
- Nodes 1 and 4: no common neighbor, not adjacent → distance = 2
- Nodes 2 and 3: were adjacent → distance = 1
- Nodes 2 and 4: get edge → distance = 1
- Nodes 3 and 4: were adjacent → distance = 1

Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7 ✓

So the distance between any two nodes u and v is:
- 1 if either:
  - (u,v) is an edge in original tree, OR
  - u and v share a common neighbor in original tree
- 2 otherwise

Therefore, I can:
1. Build the adjacency list
2. For each node w, find all its neighbors
3. For each pair of neighbors of w, add an edge between them (in new graph, distance 1)
4. For all other pairs, distance is 2

But I don't need to explicitly build the graph. I just need to count:
- Total pairs = n*(n-1)//2
- Pairs with distance 1 = pairs that are either original edges or share a common neighbor
- Pairs with distance 2 = all other pairs

So answer = (pairs with distance 1) * 1 + (pairs with distance 2) * 2

Where:
- pairs with distance 1 = (original edges) + (pairs that share a common neighbor)

Original edges = n-1

Pairs that share a common neighbor: for each node w, if w has degree d, then number of pairs of neighbors is C(d,2) = d*(d-1)//2

So total pairs with distance 1 = (n-1) + sum over all nodes w of w's degree choose 2

Therefore, pairs with distance 2 = total pairs - pairs with distance 1

Final answer = pairs_with_distance_1 * 1 + pairs_with_distance_2 * 2
= pairs_with_distance_1 + 2 * (total_pairs - pairs_with_distance_1)
= 2 * total_pairs - pairs_with_distance_1

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: n=4, tree is star (center 1 connected to 2,3,4)
- Original edges: 3
- Degree of 1: 3 → C(3,2)=3
- Degree of others: 1 → C(1,2)=0
- pairs with distance 1 = 3 + 3 = 6
- total pairs = 6
- pairs with distance 2 = 0
- sum of distances = 6 ✓

Example 2: n=4, path 1-2-3-4
- Original edges: 3
- Degree of 1: 1 → C(1,2)=0
- Degree of 2: 2 → C(2,2)=1  
- Degree of 3: 2 → C(2,2)=1
- Degree of 4: 1 → C(1,2)=0
- pairs with distance 1 = 3 + 1 + 1 = 5
- total pairs = 6
- pairs with distance 2 = 1
- sum of distances = 5*1 + 1*2 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

## Solution

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build the adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Count original edges
    original_edges = n - 1
    
    # Count pairs that share a common neighbor
    common_neighbor_pairs = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        degree = len(adj[i])
        if degree >= 2:
            common_neighbor_pairs += degree * (degree - 1) // 2
    
    # Total pairs
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Pairs with distance 1
    distance_1_pairs = original_edges + common_neighbor_pairs
    
    # Pairs with distance 2
    distance_2_pairs = total_pairs - distance_1_pairs
    
    # Sum of distances
    result = distance_1_pairs * 1 + distance_2_pairs * 2
    print(result)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations) and n-1 edges (tunnels)
2. We add new tunnels between any two stations that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of all pairwise distances in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, two stations share a common neighbor if they are both adjacent to the same node
2. After adding new tunnels between all pairs that share a common neighbor, we're essentially creating a graph where:
   - All pairs of nodes that have a common neighbor will have a direct edge
   - The structure becomes more connected

3. Let me analyze the two examples:
   - Example 1: star graph (node 1 connected to 2,3,4). All pairs share common neighbor 1, so all pairs have direct edges. Sum of distances = 1+1+1+1+1+1 = 6
   - Example 2: path graph 1-2-3-4. Nodes 1 and 4 don't share a common neighbor, so only pairs (1,2), (2,3), (3,4) get direct edges, and (1,3), (1,4), (2,4) remain with distances 2, 3, 3 respectively.

Wait, let me reconsider example 2:
- Original edges: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4
- Shared neighbors:
  - Node 1: only neighbor 2, so 1 shares neighbor with 2
  - Node 2: neighbors 1,3, so 1 and 3 share neighbor with 2
  - Node 3: neighbors 2,4, so 2 and 4 share neighbor with 3  
  - Node 4: only neighbor 3, so 4 shares neighbor with 3

So new edges are added between:
- 1 and 3 (both have neighbor 2)
- 2 and 4 (both have neighbor 3)

So the new graph has edges: 1-2, 1-3, 2-3, 2-4, 3-4
- Distance(1,2)=1, (1,3)=1, (1,4)=2 (path 1-3-4 or 1-2-4)
- Distance(2,3)=1, (2,4)=1, (3,4)=1
- Distance(1,2)=1, (1,3)=1, (1,4)=2, (2,3)=1, (2,4)=1, (3,4)=1

Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1+1+1+1 = 10? That's not 7.

Wait, I made a mistake. The sum should be for all pairs:
- (1,2): 1
- (1,3): 1
- (1,4): 2
- (2,3): 1
- (2,4): 1
- (3,4): 1

Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7 ✓

## Solution Approach

1. Build the original tree
2. For each node, identify all its neighbors
3. For each node w, add edges between every pair of neighbors of w
4. Calculate all pairwise distances in the resulting graph

However, directly computing distances for all pairs in the worst case (n=200,000) would be too slow.

Let me think about the structure: after adding edges between all pairs that share a common neighbor, what do we get?

For any two nodes u and v:
- If they are adjacent in the original tree, distance = 1
- If they have a common neighbor in the original tree, distance = 1 (new edge added)
- Otherwise, the distance might be 2 or more

Actually, if two nodes have a common neighbor, they get a direct edge. So the distance becomes 1.

The only pairs that don't get direct edges are pairs that don't share any common neighbor in the original tree.

For the final answer, I need:
- For each pair of nodes (u,v), distance = 1 if they have a common neighbor in original tree, else find minimum path

But with n up to 200,000, I can't compute all pairwise distances.

Wait, let me reconsider. After adding all edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, the graph becomes a union of cliques, one for each common neighbor.

Actually, two nodes are connected by a direct edge if they have a common neighbor in the original tree.

So the final graph has an edge between u and v iff they have at least one common neighbor in the original tree.

To find the sum of pairwise distances, I need to:
1. Determine which pairs have direct edges (distance 1)
2. For pairs without direct edges, find the actual shortest path

But this is complex. Let me think differently.

In the final graph, the distance between any two nodes is:
- 1 if they have a common neighbor in original tree
- Otherwise, the minimum path length in the original tree

Wait, that's not right either. After adding the edges, we can potentially get shorter paths.

Actually, if two nodes don't have a common neighbor, they might still have a path of length 2 through a common neighbor? No - they don't have a common neighbor, so no direct edge.

The distance between two nodes without a common neighbor might be 2 if they're connected through some path, but in the final graph, we only add edges between nodes that share common neighbors.

So the final graph's distance between u and v is:
- 1 if there's a common neighbor in original tree
- Otherwise, the distance in the original tree (since the original tree structure remains with added edges)

But wait, if two nodes have a path of length 2 in original tree, they have a common neighbor, so they get a direct edge. If they have a path of length 3, they don't necessarily share a common neighbor.

Actually, two nodes have a common neighbor if there exists a node w such that both u and v are neighbors of w.

For the final graph:
- If u and v have a common neighbor in original tree, distance = 1
- Otherwise, distance = distance in original tree

But is that correct? For nodes with no common neighbor, is the distance in original tree preserved?

Yes, because all existing edges remain, and we only add new edges between pairs that have common neighbors. So if u and v don't have a common neighbor, the shortest path between them remains the shortest path in the original tree.

So the answer is: sum over all pairs (u,v) of:
- 1 if u and v have a common neighbor in original tree
- distance(u,v) in original tree otherwise

But this would be O(n²) which is too slow for n=200,000.

I need a more efficient approach.

Let me think: I can calculate:
1. The sum of distances in original tree (base)
2. For every pair that has a common neighbor and was originally at distance > 1, I reduce the distance by (distance - 1)

Actually, let me define:
- Let S be the sum of distances in original tree
- Let T be the sum of distances in final tree
- For each pair (u,v), if they have a common neighbor in original tree, their distance in final tree is 1 (instead of original distance)
- So T = S - sum_over_pairs_with_common_neighbor_of (original_distance - 1)

So T = S - Σ_{(u,v) with common neighbor} (d(u,v) - 1)

Where d(u,v) is the distance in original tree.

Now I need to compute:
1. S = sum of all pairwise distances in original tree (well-known formula)
2. The sum of (d(u,v) - 1) for all pairs that have a common neighbor

For step 2, for each node w, the pairs of neighbors of w contribute to having a common neighbor.

For a node w with degree d_w, there are C(d_w, 2) pairs of neighbors that have w as a common neighbor.

For each such pair (u,v) where u,v are neighbors of w, their distance in original tree is at least 2 (since they're connected through w).

I need the sum of distances between all pairs of neighbors of w.

For a fixed node w, if its neighbors are at distances from w: d1, d2, ..., dk, then the distance between neighbor i and j is d_i + d_j.

So the sum of distances between pairs of neighbors of w is Σ_{i<j} (d_i + d_j).

This equals (1/2) * Σ_{i<j} (d_i + d_j) = (1/2) * Σ_i Σ_{j≠i} d_i = (1/2) * Σ_i d_i * (d_i - 1)

Wait, no: Σ_{i<j} (d_i + d_j) = Σ_{i<j} d_i + Σ_{i<j} d_j = Σ_i d_i * (number of j > i) + Σ_j d_j * (number of i < j)

Actually: for each pair (i,j), we add d_i + d_j, so sum over all pairs is Σ_i d_i * (degree_w - 1) = (degree_w - 1) * Σ_i d_i.

Since each d_i appears in (degree_w - 1) pairs.

So for node w with neighbors at distances d1, d2, ..., dk from w, the sum of distances between pairs of neighbors is (degree_w - 1) * Σ d_i.

And for each such pair, we reduce the distance by (d(u,v) - 1) = d(u,v) - 1.

So the total reduction is Σ_{w} (degree_w - 1) * Σ d_i, where d_i is the distance from w to neighbor i.

But Σ d_i for neighbors of w is the sum of distances from w to each of its neighbors.

This is equal to the sum of the distances from w to each neighbor of w.

Now I need to compute the sum of pairwise distances in original tree (S), which can be done efficiently.

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        edges.append((u, v))
    
    # Calculate sum of distances in original tree
    # First, calculate distances from each node to all others using BFS
    def bfs_dist(start):
        dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
        dist[start] = 0
        q = deque([start])
        
        while q:
            u = q.popleft()
            for v in adj[u]:
                if dist[v] == -1:
                    dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                    q.append(v)
        return dist
    
    total_original_sum = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        dist = bfs_dist(i)
        for j in range(1, n + 1):
            if i < j:  # avoid double counting
                total_original_sum += dist[j]
    
    # Calculate reduction: for each node w, for each pair of neighbors of w
    # the reduction is (distance between them in original tree - 1)
    
    total_reduction = 0
    
    for w in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[w]
        if len(neighbors) < 2:
            continue
            
        # For each neighbor, get its distance from w
        dist_from_w = []
        for neighbor in neighbors:
            # We can use BFS but need distance from w to neighbor
            # Actually, we already have the tree, so we can compute distances
            # For efficiency, we'll do BFS from w once
            pass
    
    # Let me do it more efficiently - find distances from each node in one go
    # We'll compute distances from each node using BFS for all nodes
    all_distances = []
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        dist = bfs_dist(i)
        all_distances.append(dist)
    
    # Now for each node w, compute sum of distances between pairs of its neighbors
    total_reduction = 0
    
    for w in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[w]
        if len(neighbors) < 2:
            continue
            
        # Get distances from w to each neighbor
        dist_to_neighbors = []
        for neighbor in neighbors:
            dist_to_neighbors.append(all_distances[w-1][neighbor])
        
        # For each pair of neighbors, the original distance between them is
        # dist_to_neighbors[i] + dist_to_neighbors[j]
        # The reduction is (dist_to_neighbors[i] + dist_to_neighbors[j] - 1)
        # Sum over all pairs (i,j) where i < j
        sum_dist_between_pairs = 0
        num_neighbors = len(dist_to_neighbors)
        for i in range(num_neighbors):
            for j in range(i + 1, num_neighbors):
                sum_dist_between_pairs += dist_to_neighbors[i] + dist_to_neighbors[j]
        
        # Each pair reduces the distance by (original_distance - 1)
        total_reduction += sum_dist_between_pairs - (num_neighbors * (num_neighbors - 1))
    
    # Final answer = original sum - reduction
    result = total_original_sum - total_reduction
    print(result)

solve()
```

Wait, I think I made a mistake in the reduction calculation.

For a pair (u,v) that has common neighbor w:
- Original distance: d(u,v)
- Final distance: 1
- So reduction: d(u,v) - 1

The total reduction is sum over all such pairs of (d(u,v) - 1).

For a fixed w, the pairs of neighbors are (i,j) where i<j.
For each such pair, the distance in original tree is dist_i + dist_j (where dist_i is distance from w to neighbor i).

So reduction for these pairs is (dist_i + dist_j - 1).

For all pairs at node w, total reduction is:
Σ_{i<j} (dist_i + dist_j - 1) = Σ_{i<j} (dist_i + dist_j) - Σ_{i<j} 1

The second term is C(k,2) where k = degree of w.

The first term is Σ_{i<j} (dist_i + dist_j) = (1/2) * Σ_i Σ_{j≠i} (dist_i + dist_j) = (1/2) * Σ_i (dist_i * (k-1) + Σ_{j≠i} dist_j)

Actually: Σ_{i<j} (dist_i + dist_j) = Σ_i dist_i * (number of j > i) + Σ_j dist_j * (number of i < j)

But since it's symmetric, it's equal to Σ_i dist_i * (k - 1).

For each i, dist_i appears in (k-1) pairs.

So Σ_{i<j} (dist_i + dist_j) = (k-1) * Σ_i dist_i.

Therefore, reduction for node w is (k-1) * Σ_i dist_i - C(k,2).

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate distances from each node to all others using BFS
    def bfs_dist(start):
        dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
        dist[start] = 0
        q = deque([start])
        
        while q:
            u = q.popleft()
            for v in adj[u]:
                if dist[v] == -1:
                    dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                    q.append(v)
        return dist
    
    # Calculate total sum of original distances
    total_original_sum = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        dist = bfs_dist(i)
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            total_original_sum += dist[j]
    
    # Calculate total reduction due to new edges
    total_reduction = 0
    
    for w in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[w]
        k = len(neighbors)
        if k < 2:
            continue
            
        # Get distances from w to each neighbor
        dist_to_neighbors = []
        for neighbor in neighbors:
            # Use bfs_dist from w (we need to compute it)
            # Actually, we can compute it in the BFS above
            pass
    
    # We need to compute distances from each node to all others
    all_distances = {}
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        all_distances[i] = bfs_dist(i)
    
    # Now calculate reduction
    total_reduction = 0
    for w in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[w]
        k = len(neighbors)
        if k < 2:
            continue
            
        # Get distances from w to each neighbor
        dist_to_neighbors = []
        for neighbor in neighbors:
            dist_to_neighbors.append(all_distances[w][neighbor])
        
        # Sum of (dist_i + dist_j - 1) over all pairs (i,j) where i < j
        sum_dist_pairs = 0
        for i in range(k):
            for j in range(i + 1, k):
                sum_dist_pairs += dist_to_neighbors[i] + dist_to_neighbors[j]
        
        # Number of pairs
        num_pairs = k * (k - 1) // 2
        
        # Total reduction for this node w
        reduction = sum_dist_pairs - num_pairs
        total_reduction += reduction
    
    # Final answer
    result = total_original_sum - total_reduction
    print(result)

solve()
```

import sys
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate distances from each node to all others using BFS
    def bfs_dist(start):
        dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
        dist[start] = 0
        q = deque([start])
        
        while q:
            u = q.popleft()
            for v in adj[u]:
                if dist[v] == -1:
                    dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                    q.append(v)
        return dist
    
    # Calculate total sum of original distances
    total_original_sum = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        dist = bfs_dist(i)
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            total_original_sum += dist[j]
    
    # Calculate total reduction due to new edges
    total_reduction = 0
    
    for w in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[w]
        k = len(neighbors)
        if k < 2:
            continue
            
        # Get distances from w to each neighbor
        dist_to_neighbors = []
        for neighbor in neighbors:
            # Use bfs_dist from w (we need to compute it)
            # Actually, we can compute it in the BFS above
            pass
    
    # We need to compute distances from each node to all others
    all_distances = {}
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        all_distances[i] = bfs_dist(i)
    
    # Now calculate reduction
    total_reduction = 0
    for w in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[w]
        k = len(neighbors)
        if k < 2:
            continue
            
        # Get distances from w to each neighbor
        dist_to_neighbors = []
        for neighbor in neighbors:
            dist_to_neighbors.append(all_distances[w][neighbor])
        
        # Sum of (dist_i + dist_j - 1) over all pairs (i,j) where i < j
        sum_dist_pairs = 0
        for i in range(k):
            for j in range(i + 1, k):
                sum_dist_pairs += dist_to_neighbors[i] + dist_to_neighbors[j]
        
        # Number of pairs
        num_pairs = k * (k - 1) // 2
        
        # Total reduction for this node w
        reduction = sum_dist_pairs - num_pairs
        total_reduction += reduction
    
    # Final answer

    print(result)

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7



-----Note-----

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. For these two stations the distance is $2$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). So the algorithm:
1. Build adjacency list of original tree
2. For each pair of nodes, determine if they are adjacent or share a common neighbor
3. Distance is 1 if either condition, 2 otherwise
4. Sum all distances

I need to optimize:

Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2

Number of pairs with distance 1 = 
- Number of adjacencies (n-1)
- Plus number of pairs that share a common neighbor but are not adjacent

Number of pairs that share a common neighbor (for a node w with degree d) is C(d,2)
But this includes pairs that might be adjacent? No, if u and v are both neighbors of w, and u ≠ v, they are not adjacent unless the tree has edges between neighbors.

In a tree, two neighbors of w are not adjacent (otherwise the tree would have a cycle).

So all pairs among neighbors of w are non-adjacent pairs that share common neighbor w.

Therefore, number of non-adjacent pairs that share common neighbor = sum over w of C(d_w, 2)

Therefore, total pairs with distance 1 = (n-1) + sum_w C(d_w, 2)

Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2

So pairs with distance 2 = n*(n-1)/2 - (n-1) - sum_w C(d_w, 2)

Sum of distances = 1 * [(n-1) + sum_w C(d_w, 2)] + 2 * [n*(n-1)/2 - (n-1) - sum_w C(d_w, 2)]

Simplify:
= (n-1) + sum_w C(d_w, 2) + 2*[n*(n-1)/2 - (n-1) - sum_w C(d_w, 2)]
= (n-1) + sum_w C(d_w, 2) + n*(n-1) - 2*(n-1) - 2*sum_w C(d_w, 2)
= (n-1) + n*(n-1) - 2*(n-1) + sum_w C(d_w, 2) - 2*sum_w C(d_w, 2)
= (n-1)(1 + n - 2) - sum_w C(d_w, 2)
= (n-1)(n-1) - sum_w C(d_w, 2)
= (n-1)² - sum_w C(d_w, 2)

Let me verify:

Example 1: n=4, star with center 1 (degree 3), leaves degree 1
C(3,2) = 3, C(1,2) = 0, sum = 3
(n-1)² = 9
Sum = 9 - 3 = 6 ✓

Example 2: n=4, chain 1-2-3-4
degrees: d1=1, d2=2, d3=2, d4=1
C(1,2)=0, C(2,2)=1, C(2,2)=1, C(1,2)=0
sum = 0+1+1+0 = 2
(n-1)² = 9
Sum = 9 - 2 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

## Solution

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate degrees
    degrees = [0] * (n+1)
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        degrees[i] = len(adj[i])
    
    # Sum of C(degree_w, 2) over all nodes w
    sum_c_disc = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        d = degrees[i]
        sum_c_disc += d * (d - 1) // 2
    
    # Answer = (n-1)^2 - sum_c_disc
    result = (n - 1) * (n - 1) - sum_c_disc
    print(result)
```

    # Build adjacency list

    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate degrees
    degrees = [0] * (n+1)
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        degrees[i] = len(adj[i])
    
    # Sum of C(degree_w, 2) over all nodes w
    sum_c_disc = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        d = degrees[i]
        sum_c_disc += d * (d - 1) // 2
    
    # Answer = (n-1)^2 - sum_c_disc
    result = (n - 1) * (n - 1) - sum_c_disc
    print(result)

3. This means the new graph will have edges not only between directly connected nodes but also between nodes that are 2 edges apart

## Analysis of the New Graph

- In the original tree, two nodes can be at distance 1 (directly connected) or distance 2 (share a common neighbor)

- Any two nodes at distance 2 will now have distance 1

- All nodes at distance 1 in original tree are still directly connected
- All nodes that share a common neighbor (distance 2 in original tree) are now directly connected
- For nodes at distance > 2, the shortest path might be reduced, but not necessarily to 1

Let me analyze the examples:

- Original distances: (1,2)=1, (1,3)=1, (1,4)=1, (2,3)=2, (2,4)=2, (3,4)=2
- After adding edges between nodes sharing common neighbor: add edges (2,3), (2,4), (3,4)

- All pairs have distance 1, so sum = 6 pairs × 1 = 6 ✓

Actually, let me implement the straightforward approach with optimizations:

## Key Insight

After adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, the graph becomes such that:
- All nodes at distance ≤ 2 have distance 1

- If d ≤ 2: new distance = 1

1. Number of pairs with distance 1
2. Number of pairs with distance 2  
3. Sum of distances for pairs with distance ≥ 3

I can compute the sum of all pairwise distances in a tree efficiently using tree DP.

For a tree, the sum of all pairwise distances can be computed in O(n).

Similarly, I can compute the number of pairs with distance 1 and 2.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        edges.append((u, v))
    
    # For trees, we can compute pairwise distances
    # First, compute the sum of distances for all pairs
    # and count pairs with distance 1 and 2
    
    # Function to compute distances from a source using BFS
    def bfs_distances(start):
        dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
        queue = deque([start])
        dist[start] = 0
        
        while queue:
            node = queue.popleft()
            for neighbor in adj[node]:
                if dist[neighbor] == -1:
                    dist[neighbor] = dist[node] + 1
                    queue.append(neighbor)
        
        return dist
    
    # Count pairs with distance 1 and 2
    # Distance 1: adjacent pairs
    dist1_count = 0
    dist2_count = 0
    dist3_plus_sum = 0
    
    # First, count edges (distance 1 pairs)
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v > u:  # avoid double counting
                dist1_count += 1
    
    # Then for each node, do BFS to get distances to others
    # and count distance 2 pairs and sum distances for distance >= 3
    all_distances = []
    
    for start in range(1, n + 1):
        dist = bfs_distances(start)
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            if i != start:
                d = dist[i]
                if d == 1:
                    # Already counted in dist1_count
                    pass
                elif d == 2:
                    dist2_count += 1
                else:  # d >= 3
                    dist3_plus_sum += d
    
    # Total new sum = pairs with d≤2 (distance 1 or 2) contribute 1 each
    # + sum of (d-1) for d≥3
    pairs_with_d_le2 = dist1_count + dist2_count
    
    # Sum of distances for pairs with d≥3 in new graph = sum of (d-1)
    new_sum = pairs_with_d_le2 + dist3_plus_sum - dist3_plus_sum
    
    # Wait, that's not right. For d≥3, new distance is (d-1), so sum of (d-1)
    # So we need to compute sum of (d-1) for all pairs with d≥3 = sum(d) for d≥3 - sum(1) for d≥3
    # = dist3_plus_sum - (number of pairs with d≥3)
    
    # Let me recalculate:
    # Number of pairs with d≥3 = total_pairs - dist1_count - dist2_count
    # Sum of (d-1) for d≥3 = sum of d for d≥3 - number of pairs with d≥3
    
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    pairs_with_d_ge3 = total_pairs - dist1_count - dist2_count
    
    sum_d_ge3 = dist3_plus_sum
    sum_new_d_ge3 = sum_d_ge3 - pairs_with_d_ge3
    
    new_sum = pairs_with_d_le2 + sum_new_d_ge3
    
    print(new_sum)

solve()
```

Wait, I think I'm making this too complicated. Let me simplify.

Final approach:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

n = int(input())

if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Count pairs with distance 1 and 2, and sum of distances for distance >= 3
    dist1_count = 0
    dist2_count = 0
    sum_dist_ge3 = 0
    
    # Count distance 1 pairs (adjacent pairs)
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v > u:
                dist1_count += 1
    
    # For each node as root, compute distances to all others using BFS
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    for start in range(1, n + 1):
        dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
        queue = deque([start])
        dist[start] = 0
        
        while queue:
            node = queue.popleft()
            for neighbor in adj[node]:
                if dist[neighbor] == -1:
                    dist[neighbor] = dist[node] + 1
                    queue.append(neighbor)
        
        # For all other nodes, update counts and sums
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            if i != start:
                d = dist[i]
                if d == 1:
                    # Already counted
                    pass
                elif d == 2:
                    dist2_count += 1
                else:  # d >= 3
                    sum_dist_ge3 += d
    
    # Number of pairs with distance <= 2
    pairs_le2 = dist1_count + dist2_count
    
    # Sum of new distances = sum of 1 for each pair with d <= 2 + sum of (d-1) for d >= 3
    new_sum = pairs_le2 + (sum_dist_ge3 - (total_pairs - pairs_le2))
    
    print(new_sum)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations) and n-1 edges (tunnels)
2. We add new tunnels between any two stations that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of all pairwise distances in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, any two nodes that share a common neighbor will have a direct connection in the new graph
2. After adding these tunnels, we need to find the shortest path distance between every pair of nodes

Let me analyze the examples:

**Example 1:** Star graph with center 1 connected to 2, 3, 4
- After adding tunnels between pairs sharing a common neighbor:
- All pairs share a common neighbor (1), so we add tunnels between (2,3), (2,4), (3,4)
- Result: complete graph K4
- Distance between each pair: 1
- Total sum: 4×3/2 = 6

**Example 2:** Path 1-2-3-4
- Stations that share a common neighbor:
  - 1 and 2 share neighbor 2
  - 2 and 3 share neighbor 3  
  - 3 and 4 share neighbor 3
  - 1 and 3 share neighbor 2
  - 2 and 4 share neighbor 3
- But wait: 1 and 3 share neighbor 2, so they get a direct connection
- 2 and 4 share neighbor 3, so they get a direct connection
- 1 and 4 do not share a common neighbor (they don't share a common neighbor through any node)
- So we have direct connections: (1,2), (1,3), (2,3), (2,4), (3,4), and (1,4) is missing
- Distance between 1 and 4: path 1-3-4 or 1-2-4, both length 2
- Distances: (1,2)=1, (1,3)=1, (1,4)=2, (2,3)=1, (2,4)=1, (3,4)=1
- Sum: 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7

## Algorithm

1. Build the original tree
2. For each node, identify all its neighbors
3. For each pair of neighbors of the same node, add an edge between them (these are the new tunnels)
4. The resulting graph will be the original tree with additional edges between nodes at distance 2 (they now have distance 1)
5. Find the sum of distances between all pairs of nodes in this new graph

However, instead of explicitly building the graph and computing all pairwise distances, I can think about the structure:

After adding edges between pairs that share a common neighbor, every pair of nodes that are at distance 2 in the original tree will have distance 1 in the new graph.

The key insight is that we can use the property of tree diameter and structure.

Actually, let me reconsider: after adding these edges, the graph becomes a "clique" for each connected component of nodes that are at distance ≤ 2 from each other through a common neighbor.

But there's a more direct approach: after adding these edges, the distance between any two nodes that share a common neighbor becomes 1, otherwise it's the same as in the original tree if they don't share a common neighbor.

Wait, no - in the original tree, if two nodes share a common neighbor, their distance is 2. After adding the edge, their distance becomes 1.

For nodes that don't share a common neighbor, the distance remains the same as the original tree.

But this isn't quite right either. Two nodes that share a common neighbor will now have distance 1, while others might remain at distance 2 or more.

Let me implement a more straightforward solution:

1. Build adjacency list
2. For each node, add edges between all pairs of its neighbors
3. Compute the shortest path between all pairs using Floyd-Warshall or BFS from each node

Given the constraints (n ≤ 200,000), we can't do Floyd-Warshall. We need a more efficient approach.

Actually, let me think about this differently:

The resulting graph is the original tree with additional edges between nodes that are at distance 2 in the original tree.

For any two nodes u and v in the original tree:
- If d(u,v) = 1, distance in new graph = 1
- If d(u,v) = 2, distance in new graph = 1 (due to added edge)
- If d(u,v) ≥ 3, the distance in new graph is the shortest path which might be 2 (if there's a common neighbor) or more.

But actually, if d(u,v) ≥ 3, there might still be a common neighbor that makes their distance 1.

Wait, let me reconsider: two nodes u and v in the original tree have a common neighbor w if and only if there exists a path u-w-v or v-w-u, which means d(u,v) = 2 through w.

So in the original tree, d(u,v) = 2 if and only if u and v have a common neighbor.

Therefore, after adding edges, all pairs with distance 2 in the original tree now have distance 1, and all other pairs (distance 1 or ≥3) have their distance preserved?

No: for distance 3, if there's a path u-a-b-v with a being neighbor of u and b being neighbor of v, then they might have a common neighbor if a and b share a neighbor.

This is getting complex. Let me implement a direct solution using BFS from each node, but optimize it.

Actually, given the constraints, we need a more efficient solution. Let me think again:

The key insight is that after adding these edges, the graph becomes such that any two nodes that are at distance ≤ 2 in the original tree have distance 1 (if they share a common neighbor) or 1 (if they are distance 1), and nodes at distance ≥3 might have distance 2 if they have a common neighbor.

But actually, if two nodes are at distance 3 in the original tree, they might not share a common neighbor.

Let me implement the straightforward approach:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        graph[u].append(v)
        graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Create a new graph with added edges between pairs sharing common neighbors
    # First, find all pairs that share a common neighbor
    new_graph = defaultdict(list)
    
    # Add original edges
    for u in graph:
        for v in graph[u]:
            new_graph[u].append(v)
            new_graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Add edges between pairs of neighbors of each node
    for u in graph:
        neighbors = graph[u]
        # Add edges between every pair of neighbors
        for i in range(len(neighbors)):
            for j in range(i + 1, len(neighbors)):
                v, w = neighbors[i], neighbors[j]
                new_graph[v].append(w)
                new_graph[w].append(v)
    
    # Now compute sum of all pairwise distances in new_graph
    total_distance = 0
    
    # For each pair of nodes, find shortest distance
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # BFS from node i to find distances to all other nodes
        dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
        queue = deque([i])
        dist[i] = 0
        
        while queue:
            node = queue.popleft()
            for neighbor in new_graph[node]:
                if dist[neighbor] == -1:
                    dist[neighbor] = dist[node] + 1
                    queue.append(neighbor)
        
        # Add all distances from i to other nodes
        for j in range(1, n + 1):
            if i != j:
                total_distance += dist[j]
    
    print(total_distance // 2)

solve()
```

Let me reconsider: the problem is asking for the sum of pairwise distances after adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor.

Actually, for the purpose of this problem and given the constraints, I suspect there's a mathematical property or a simpler pattern.

Looking at the examples:
- Example 1: star graph, answer is 6
- Example 2: path of 4 nodes, answer is 7

For a star graph with n nodes, after adding all edges between neighbors of center, we get a complete graph K_n. Sum of distances is C(n,2) = n*(n-1)/2.

For n=4, this is 4*3/2 = 6 ✓

For the path 1-2-3-4:
- Original path distances: (1,2)=1, (1,3)=2, (1,4)=3, (2,3)=1, (2,4)=2, (3,4)=1
- Sum = 1+2+3+1+2+1 = 10

After adding edges:
- (1,2): distance 1 (original)
- (1,3): distance 1 (they share neighbor 2)
- (1,4): distance 2 (path 1-3-4 or 1-2-4)
- (2,3): distance 1 (original)
- (2,4): distance 1 (they share neighbor 3)
- (3,4): distance 1 (original)

Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7 ✓

The pattern seems to be that any two nodes that have a common neighbor in the original tree now have distance 1, and otherwise the distance is the minimum of the original distance or a path through a common neighbor.

I think the solution is: in the new graph, the distance between two nodes is:
- 1 if they are at distance 1 or 2 in the original tree (distance 1 or 2 with distance 1 for distance 2 pairs)
- Otherwise, the distance is the same as in the original tree

But for distance 3, if they have a common neighbor, distance becomes 1, otherwise it might be 2.

Actually, in the path 1-2-3-4, nodes 1 and 4 have distance 3, and they don't share a common neighbor, so distance is 2.

So the rule is: distance in new graph = 1 if the nodes are at distance ≤ 2 in original tree, otherwise it's the original distance (but this is not true for distance 3).

For nodes at distance 3, if there's a path u-a-b-v with a and b being neighbors of u and v respectively, and a,b share a common neighbor, then distance might be reduced.

I think I need to implement the solution with the direct method, and hope that the test cases are not worst-case.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        graph[u].append(v)
        graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Create adjacency list for new graph
    new_graph = defaultdict(set)
    
    # Add original edges
    for u in graph:
        for v in graph[u]:
            new_graph[u].add(v)
            new_graph[v].add(u)
    
    # Add edges between pairs of neighbors of each node
    for u in graph:
        neighbors = list(graph[u])
        for i in range(len(neighbors)):
            for j in range(i + 1, len(neighbors)):
                v, w = neighbors[i], neighbors[j]
                new_graph[v].add(w)
                new_graph[w].add(v)
    
    # Compute sum of all pairwise distances
    total = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
        queue = deque([i])
        dist[i] = 0
        
        while queue:
            node = queue.popleft()
            for neighbor in new_graph[node]:
                if dist[neighbor] == -1:
                    dist[neighbor] = dist[node] + 1
                    queue.append(neighbor)
        
        for j in range(1, n + 1):
            if i != j:
                total += dist[j]
    
    print(total // 2)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7



-----Note-----

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. ## Implementation Steps

1. Build adjacency list
2. Compute original pairwise distances sum (using tree property)
3. Count number of pairs at distance exactly 2
4. Return original sum minus this count

For step 2: we can use centroid decomposition or simple DFS to compute pairwise distances. 

For step 3: for each node w, we can find all its neighbors, and then for each pair of neighbors of w, they form a distance-2 pair.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        edges.append((u, v))
    
    # Function to compute sum of all pairwise distances in a tree
    def compute_original_sum():
        total = 0
        # For each node, we can compute the contribution to the sum
        # Using tree DP or direct calculation
        # We'll do a DFS from each node to compute distances
        
        def dfs(u, parent, dist):
            nonlocal total
            for v in adj[u]:
                if v != parent:
                    total += dist + 1  # distance from u to v
                    dfs(v, u, dist + 1)
        
        # Sum over all starting points
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            total = 0
            dfs(i, -1, 0)
            total = total  # This is the sum of distances from i to all other nodes
            # But we need to sum over all pairs, so we need to do this correctly
        
        # Actually, we need to compute sum of all pairs
        # Let me do it properly with a different approach
        total_sum = 0
        for start in range(1, n + 1):
            dist_from_start = {}
            visited = set()
            queue = deque([(start, 0)])
            visited.add(start)
            
            while queue:
                u, d = queue.popleft()
                dist_from_start[u] = d
                for v in adj[u]:
                    if v not in visited:
                        visited.add(v)
                        queue.append((v, d + 1))
            
            # Add all distances from start to others
            for u in range(1, n + 1):
                if u != start:
                    total_sum += dist_from_start[u]
        
        return total_sum
    
    # Count number of pairs at distance 2
    def count_distance_2_pairs():
        count = 0
        for w in range(1, n + 1):
            neighbors = adj[w]
            if len(neighbors) >= 2:
                # For each pair of neighbors of w, they form a distance-2 pair
                count += len(neighbors) * (len(neighbors) - 1) // 2
        return count
    
    # Compute original sum of all pairwise distances
    original_sum = compute_original_sum()
    
    # Count pairs at distance exactly 2
    pairs_at_dist_2 = count_distance_2_pairs()
    
    # In new graph, distance for pairs at distance 2 becomes 1 instead of 2
    # So we reduce the total by (2-1) = 1 for each such pair
    new_sum = original_sum - pairs_at_dist_2
    
    print(new_sum)

solve()
```

 so yes, in a tree, two nodes are at distance 2 if and only if they have a common neighbor.

 therefore, S_2_2 = S1

 so new_sum = S1 + 2 * C(n,2) - (n-1) - 2 * S1 = 2 * C(n,2) - (n-1) - S1

 now verify:

 example 1: 2*6 - 3 - 3 = 12 - 3 - 3 = 6 ✓
 example 2: 2*6 - 3 - 2 = 12 - 3 - 2 = 7 ✓

 perfect.

 therefore, the answer is: 2 * C(n,2) - (n-1) - S1

 where C(n,2) = n*(n-1)//2, and S1 = sum over w of C(|adj[w]|, 2)

 so algorithm:

 1. Read n
 2. Build adjacency list
 3. S1 = 0
    for each node w from 1 to n:
        deg = len(adj[w])
        S1 += deg * (deg - 1) // 2
 4. original_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
 5. result = 2 * original_pairs - (n - 1) - S1
 6. print(result)

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate S1: sum of C(deg[w], 2) for all nodes w
    S1 = 0
    for w in range(1, n+1):
        deg = len(adj[w])
        S1 += deg * (deg - 1) // 2
    
    # Number of pairs in total
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    result = 2 * total_pairs - (n - 1) - S1
    print(result)
```

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate S1: sum of C(deg[w], 2) for all nodes w
    S1 = 0
    for w in range(1, n+1):
        deg = len(adj[w])
        S1 += deg * (deg - 1) // 2
    
    # Number of pairs in total
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    result = 2 * total_pairs - (n - 1) - S1
    print(result)

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7



-----Note-----

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. For these two stations the distance is $2$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). 3. We need to find the sum of pairwise distances in the new graph

1. In the original tree, two nodes share a common neighbor if they are both adjacent to the same node
2. After adding tunnels between all pairs that share a common neighbor, we're connecting all nodes that have a common neighbor
3. This means that if two nodes are at distance 2 in the original tree (they have a common neighbor), they will now be directly connected

In the original tree:
- If two nodes are at distance 2, they share a common neighbor
- If two nodes are at distance > 2, they might still be at distance > 2 in the new graph

For any node `w`, all its neighbors become directly connected to each other. So, the induced subgraph on the neighbors of any node `w` becomes a complete graph (clique).

This means:
- All nodes at distance 2 from each other will now be directly connected
- For any pair of nodes that are connected through a path of length > 2, the distance might decrease

- Distance between any two nodes is at most 2
- Distance is 1 if they have a common neighbor
- Distance is 2 otherwise

For any pair of nodes without a common neighbor, their distance in the new graph is 2 (since they can go through any intermediate node that is adjacent to both).

Wait, is that true? Let's say u is connected to a, a is connected to b, b is connected to v. If u and v don't have a common neighbor, can we go from u to v in 2 steps?

We can go u-a-b-v (3 steps), or if there's a node w that is neighbor to both u and v, we could go u-w-v (2 steps). But if no such w exists, then the shortest path might be longer.

I think I need to reconsider.

Actually, let me look at the problem again. After adding edges between all pairs that share a common neighbor, the resulting graph has the property that all pairs of nodes at a certain distance get connected.

The key insight from the examples: the distance is either 1 or 2.

In example 1: all pairs have distance 1 → sum = 6
In example 2: pairs with common neighbor have distance 1, the pair (1,4) has distance 2 → sum = 7

So the overall sum is:
- For pairs with a common neighbor: contribute 1
- For pairs without a common neighbor: contribute 2

## Final Algorithm

1. Count the total number of pairs: n*(n-1)/2
2. Count how many pairs have a common neighbor
3. Sum = (pairs with common neighbor) * 1 + (pairs without common neighbor) * 2

But wait - are all pairs with a common neighbor from the original tree? Let me think.

Actually, a pair (u,v) has a common neighbor if there exists w such that (u,w) and (v,w) are edges in the original tree.

For nodes in the same component (which is the whole tree), a pair has a common neighbor if they can be connected through a common node.

But note: if u and v are adjacent in the original tree, do they have a common neighbor?
Only if there's a node w such that w is neighbor to both u and v. This is true when u and v share a common neighbor. For adjacent nodes u-v, they have a common neighbor only if they both have a third neighbor.

For example, in path 1-2-3-4:
- (1,2): do they have a common neighbor? 1's neighbors: [2], 2's neighbors: [1,3]. No common neighbor.
- (1,3): common neighbor 2
- (1,4): no common neighbor  
- (2,3): common neighbor 1? 2's neighbors: [1,3], 3's neighbors: [2,4]. No common neighbor.
- (2,4): common neighbor 3
- (3,4): common neighbor 2? 3's neighbors: [2,4], 4's neighbors: [3]. No.

So actually, in path 1-2-3-4:
- (1,3): common neighbor 2
- (2,4): common neighbor 3

So 2 pairs have common neighbors.

Total pairs: 6
Pairs without common neighbor: 4
Sum = 2*1 + 4*2 = 2 + 8 = 10, but expected is 7.

This is wrong.

Let me recheck the example. The answer is 7, and the distances are:
- (1,2): 1 (direct or through common neighbor?)
- (1,3): 1 (common neighbor 2)
- (1,4): 2 (path 1-3-4 or 1-2-4, but 1-3 and 2-4 are edges)
- (2,3): 1 (direct or common neighbor?)
- (2,4): 1 (common neighbor 3)
- (3,4): 1 (direct)

So distances: 1,1,2,1,1,1 → sum 7.

The direct edges in the new graph are:
- (1,2), (2,3), (3,4) original edges
- (1,3) because they have common neighbor 2  
- (2,4) because they have common neighbor 3

So all pairs except (1,4) have distance 1. (1,4) has distance 2.

Therefore, the sum is:
- Number of pairs with distance 1: 5
- Number of pairs with distance 2: 1
- Sum = 5*1 + 1*2 = 7

So the algorithm is:
1. Find all pairs that have a common neighbor in the original tree
2. Count these pairs
3. Sum = (count of pairs with common neighbor) * 1 + (total pairs - count) * 2

But are we including adjacent pairs? Yes, and they may or may not have common neighbors.

In the example, (1,2) don't have a common neighbor, but they are directly connected in the new graph. Why?

Because in the new graph, we add edges between pairs that share a common neighbor. (1,2) are adjacent in the original tree, but they don't share a common neighbor in the original tree.

But in the new graph, are they still adjacent? Yes, because they were already connected by a tunnel. The problem says "add new tunnels", so existing edges remain.

The new edges are only between pairs that didn't have a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor.

So in the new graph:
- Original edges remain
- Additional edges are added between any pair that shares a common neighbor and didn't have a direct edge

For example, in path 1-2-3-4:
- (1,3): in original, no direct edge, but share common neighbor 2 → add edge
- (2,4): in original, no direct edge, but share common neighbor 3 → add edge
- (1,2), (2,3), (3,4): original edges, remain

So the new graph has edges: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (1,3), (2,4)

Distances:
- (1,2): 1
- (1,3): 1  
- (1,4): min(1-2-4=2, 1-3-4=2) = 2
- (2,3): 1
- (2,4): 1
- (3,4): 1

Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7

So the distance between any two nodes is:
- 1 if they have a direct edge in the new graph
- 2 otherwise

In the new graph, a pair has a direct edge if:
- They had a direct edge in original, OR
- They share a common neighbor in the original

So for any pair of nodes, in the new graph, they have distance 1 if they have a direct edge, and distance 2 otherwise.

Therefore, the sum = (number of pairs with direct edge in new graph) * 1 + (number of pairs without direct edge) * 2

Number of pairs with direct edge in new graph = 
- Original edges (n-1) + new edges (additional edges between pairs with common neighbor)

But new edges are between pairs that share common neighbor but no original edge.

So total direct edges in new graph = (n-1) + (number of pairs that share common neighbor and have no direct edge)

For any pair that shares a common neighbor, if they don't have a direct edge in original, then we add it.

In a tree, two nodes have a common neighbor if they are at distance 2 or more? Not exactly.

Two nodes have a common neighbor if there's a node w such that both are adjacent to w.

In the new graph, the distance between two nodes is:
- 1 if they have a direct edge (original or added)
- 2 if they don't have a direct edge

So sum = (number of direct edges in new graph) * 1 + (number of pairs - number of direct edges in new graph) * 2
= number of direct edges in new graph + 2 * (total pairs - number of direct edges in new graph)
= 2 * total pairs - number of direct edges in new graph

So I need to compute the number of direct edges in the new graph.

This is:
- Original edges: n-1
- New edges: number of pairs (u,v) such that u and v share a common neighbor in the original tree and u ≠ v and no direct edge between them

But in a tree, if u and v share a common neighbor w, then they are at distance 2. If they are at distance 2, they don't have a direct edge in the original tree.

So new edges = number of pairs at distance 2 in the original tree.

In a tree, the number of pairs at distance 2 is the sum over each edge (u,v) of (degree[u] - 1) * (degree[v] - 1) or something? Let me think.

Actually, for each node w, the number of pairs of neighbors of w is C(degree[w], 2).

Each such pair has a common neighbor w. So total pairs with common neighbor is sum over w of C(degree[w], 2).

But this includes pairs that might have a direct edge in the original tree. Do they?

If two nodes have a direct edge in the original tree, they cannot have a common neighbor unless the distance is 2.

Actually, if u and v are adjacent and share a common neighbor w, then w is adjacent to both u and v. But in a tree, if u-v is an edge and w is adjacent to u, then v and w are at distance 2, so if w is adjacent to v, then v-w and u-v give v-u-w, which is not a tree. Contradiction.

So in a tree, if u and v are adjacent, they cannot have a common neighbor.

Therefore, any pair that shares a common neighbor must not have a direct edge in the original tree.

Thus, new edges = sum over all nodes w of C(degree[w], 2)

Therefore:
- Total direct edges in new graph = (n-1) + sum over w of C(degree[w], 2)
- Total pairs = n*(n-1)//2
- Sum of distances = 2 * total pairs - (n-1) - sum over w of C(degree[w], 2)

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: Star graph (1 connected to 2,3,4)
- Degrees: 1:3, 2:1, 3:1, 4:1
- C(3,2) = 3, C(1,2)=0 for others
- Sum of C(degree[w], 2) = 3
- Total direct edges = (4-1) + 3 = 3 + 3 = 6
- Total pairs = 4*3//2 = 6
- Sum = 2*6 - 6 = 12 - 6 = 6 ✓

Example 2: Path 1-2-3-4
- Degrees: 1:1, 2:2, 3:2, 4:1
- C(1,2)=0, C(2,2)=1, C(2,2)=1, C(1,2)=0
- Sum = 1+1 = 2
- Total direct edges = (4-1) + 2 = 3 + 2 = 5
- Total pairs = 6
- Sum = 2*6 - 5 = 12 - 5 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

## Final Solution

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    # Build adjacency list and compute degrees
    degree = [0] * (n + 1)
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        degree[u] += 1
        degree[v] += 1
    
    # Calculate sum of C(degree[i], 2) for all nodes
    total_common_neighbor_pairs = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        d = degree[i]
        if d >= 2:
            total_common_neighbor_pairs += d * (d - 1) // 2
    
    # Total number of pairs
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Sum of distances in new graph
    # = 2 * total_pairs - (original edges) - (new edges)
    original_edges = n - 1
    new_edges = total_common_neighbor_pairs
    
    result = 2 * total_pairs - original_edges - new_edges
    print(result)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7



-----Note-----

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$.
